# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.0.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### T-0357 — Chunks: Trajectory reads as "Fling", flies outward from a pattern's centre, shows its inherited aim, and can spin
- **The menu entry, card title and tooltips now say "Fling"** instead of "Trajectory" (the class and its serialized fields are unchanged, so every existing asset still loads) — the owner's own use-case walk could not find the word "fling" anywhere near the capability that does it (WALK.md row G1).
- **A new Direction mode — Burst / Fixed / Outward from pattern centre.** Outward points each spawn away from where its own producer's pattern started, so a Ring of Pyre Blasts flies outward like an explosion instead of every point sharing one cone (G3). `Trajectory.ResolveCentreDeg` is the one place this is decided; the runtime (`Apply`) and the editor preview (`ChunkPreviewSim.LaunchOf`) both call it, so they can never disagree. `PyreBlast.SpawnOne` now threads its pattern's own centre through to the Trajectory it applies.
- **Burst mode shows the inherited value on the card** — a read-only mirror of the "Burst direction" dial, with a tooltip naming exactly where that dial lives (under the preview, on the stage pane) — instead of hiding the fact that a dial elsewhere is what actually aims the flight (G4).
- **A Spin band** (degrees/sec, seeded like Speed) makes a flung blast tumble, and **an orientation tick now draws on a Fling'd blast's disc** in the preview — the only way a round footprint can show it is oriented at all — combining with Face velocity rather than replacing it (G5). `ChunkModuleRunner.Move` gained an optional `spinDegPerSec` parameter; 0 (the default) reproduces the exact old no-rotation behaviour.
- An existing Trajectory asset's old `inheritBurstDirection` choice is folded into the new `directionMode` once, in memory, the same guarded one-time-upgrade shape every other capability already uses for its own migration.
- **ZUI `ZuiLanes` bands can be dragged in time.** A host that sets `OnLaneMoved` (or passes `onLaneMoved:` to `Z.Lanes`) gets `(lane index, new start in seconds)` on every move of a drag on a band's body, clamped at 0 and rounded to 0.01 s. The control stays generic: it moves the band itself and the host decides what a start means. A drag is one Undo step (`ZuiUndoGesture`, the same collapse the sliders and the pad use), opened only once the pointer has moved 3 pt, so a plain click on a band still scrubs. The ruler, the empty track and the playhead itself (±4 pt) still scrub the playhead. The ruler's scale is frozen while a drag is under way, so a band dragged past the end does not rescale the clock under the pointer; the host's new length applies on release. A switched-off (dim) lane cannot be dragged. Draggable bands show the sideways slide cursor, their tooltip says they can be dragged, and the dragged band is outlined with its new start printed on the ruler.
- **The lane-name gutter sizes itself to its longest name** (H8). `gutterWidth` is now a maximum, and the ruler's zero follows the gutter's real width.
- **Chunks' Timing section uses both.** Dragging a band writes that capability's Delay through the window's normal `Dial` path (one Undo step per drag). The card's Delay field and the stage follow live, and no card or window is rebuilt per move. The gutter cap is 120 pt, and the section's tooltip now says what dragging a band does.

### T-0352 — Chunks: Fragment Fracture honours a Zoe's live current frame, same override as Debris Scatter
- **Fragment Fracture now cuts a Zoe's live on-screen sprite when a Zoe triggers it, instead of always taking the authored source's first frame.** It reads `ChunkModuleContext.SampleSourceOverride` — the exact plumbing Debris Scatter's `ResolvedSampleSource` already used (T-0252), forwarded automatically by `SpawnChunkFx`/`PyreChunksFx` off a Zoe's current lauminary frame — through a new `FragmentFracture.ResolveSource(in ChunkModuleContext)` overload. No second mechanism was added.
- The authored `Source`/`Fallback sprite` fields remain the fallback for a standalone (non-Zoe) burst and are still what the editor's swatch/thumbnail preview reads, unaware of any live override — the parameterless `ResolveSource()` keeps that authored-only behaviour.
- An unreadable live sprite's texture degrades exactly the way an unreadable authored source already did: `FragmentCutter.Cut` returns no pieces and `FragmentFracture`'s existing one-time warning fires (it already anticipated "frames coming from a Zoe or a Pyre" not being readable by default).
- Field tooltips on `Source`/`Fallback sprite` (runtime attribute and the Fragment Fracture card) now say a live Zoe frame outranks both when one is supplied.

### T-0350 — every AssetKit window remembers the asset it was editing when reopened
- **Closing and reopening any `ZuiAssetWindow<T>` tool (Chunks, Pyre, and every other tool on that shared base — BackSplash, Cartographer, Choreographer, DotGen, Larder, Lathe, SpriteCatalog, Mirage, SpriteFx, Tapestry, TextSplash, Zoetrope…) no longer drops to the asset browser.** `[SerializeField] asset` only survives a domain reload of a window left OPEN; a closed-then-reopened window is a brand-new instance with every field back at its default, which is why it always fell back to the browser. `ZuiAssetWindow<T>` now mirrors the last non-sub-asset it edited into `EditorPrefs`, keyed by concrete window type and this project's path, and restores it (by GUID → path → `LoadAssetAtPath<T>`) on `OnEnable`, only when the window comes up with no asset and isn't already showing the browser on purpose. Falls back silently to the browser if the remembered asset was since deleted or moved out from under the GUID. Never fights a caller's explicit `SetAsset` right after `GetWindow` (double-clicking an asset, a Mirage handoff, etc. — those run after `OnEnable` and win outright), and never fights Unity's own field serialization across a plain recompile of an already-open window.
- `Editor/AssetKit/ZuiAssetWindow.cs` only — the older IMGUI `LaubraryAssetWindow<T>` base (used by Lazor) has the identical gap but was out of this task's scope; flagged for a follow-up if wanted.

### T-0343 — Zounds: the old Zounds look is back (style sheet ships with the package)
- **Zounds draws with its original styled look again** — gradient tabs, the glowing outlined project title, bordered filter buttons, brown gradient Zound buttons, the gold volume slider. The look lives in one ZUI style sheet from the old standalone Zounds project, which never made it into the package; Zounds had been silently generating an EMPTY sheet in each project (`Assets/Zounds/ZUI Assets/ZOUNDS ZUI Style Sheet.asset`) and drawing unstyled.
- The sheet now ships at `Editor/Zounds/ZUI Assets/ZOUNDS ZUI Style Sheet.asset` (the old project's asset and GUID, `consumerName` set to `Zounds` so ZUI's own consumer auto-discovery also finds it). `ZoundsZUIBootstrap` finds it by GUID first, so it resolves under `Assets/Packages/...` and `Packages/com.lautaro.arino.laubrary/...` alike; project-local `Assets/Zounds/...` / `Assets/ZoundsData/...` sheets are now only a fallback, and the unstyled sheet is only ever created when the packaged one is genuinely missing (into the project, never the package).
- **Zounds no longer makes its sheet the ambient `ZUI.ActiveSheet`** (the bootstrap used to call `ZUI.SetDefaultActiveSheet`). Zounds' own windows already scope it by consumer name; leaving it ambient would have restyled other tools' unscoped legacy `ZUI.*` draws with Zounds' brown/gold look now that the sheet is no longer empty — e.g. `LauAssetBrowser`'s Type/Tags filter buttons.
- **Zounds Browser tab packs from the top.** The search + master-volume row centred itself with vertical `GUILayout.FlexibleSpace`s, which made that row swallow every spare pixel of window height; with only a few Zounds the toolbar and list were shoved to the bottom of the window behind big empty gaps. The row is now a plain fixed-height row (both cells are 26px), so the list sits directly under the toolbar.
- **Micro slider readouts shrink to fit instead of clipping (legacy IMGUI `ZUI.MicroSlider` / `ZUI.MicroMinMax`).** When the in-track text is wider than the track, the font steps down (to 70% of its size, never below 8px); if a "label + value" readout still doesn't fit, the bare value is shown instead, since a whole number reads better than a label clipped at both ends. Text that already fits draws exactly as before. Fixes Zounds' "Volume 57-67" / "Chance: 100" row readouts clipping under the old style sheet's larger font.
- **No new icons.** The old sheet aliases no icons; every Zounds icon already comes from the packaged `Editor/Zounds/Resources/ZoundsWindowIcons`, so nothing else was copied.
- Consumer projects: delete the stale empty `Assets/Zounds/ZUI Assets/ZOUNDS ZUI Style Sheet.asset` after updating (it no longer wins, but it is dead weight).

### T-0261 — Chunks: ZUI Envelope replaces every native CurveField
- **No native `CurveField` anywhere in Chunks — `Z.Envelope` (ZUI's own draggable multi-point editor) is now the only curve control**, project-wide policy restated with zero exceptions for AnimationCurve specifically. `DebrisScatter.sizeOverLife`/`alphaOverLife`, `FragmentFracture.alphaOverLife` and `PaletteSplash.alphaOverLife` are now `List<ZUIEnvelopePoint>` (`sizeEnvelope`/`alphaEnvelope`), sampled at runtime via `ZUIEnvelopeEvaluator.Evaluate` instead of `AnimationCurve.Evaluate`. The three editor cards (Debris Scatter, Fragment Fracture, Palette Splash) draw `Z.Envelope` instead of `Z.Curve`, wired through two new `ChunkWindow` helpers (`EnvelopeUndo`/`EnvelopeChanged`) that split the existing `Dial` undo contract across Envelope's before/after mutation hooks, since an envelope mutates its point list in place rather than handing back a new value.
- **No authored curve data is lost.** Each affected capability keeps its old `AnimationCurve` field as a `[HideInInspector]` migration source and adds `ChunkCapability.MigrateLegacyCurves()` (new virtual hook, guarded by a per-capability `curvesMigrated` flag), which densely samples the old curve into 9 evenly-spaced `ZUIEnvelopePoint`s — visually equivalent to the original Hermite curve without needing exponent-fitting. `ChunkSpec.UpgradeIfNeeded` now runs this migration on every load (cheap once done) and reports it via the existing `NeedsSaving`/`ChunkSpecMigration.UpgradeAllAssets` asset-walk, so opening the project quietly upgrades every recipe on disk exactly like the schema-version upgrade already did. Verified against a real authored asset (`Sparks.asset`): the migrated envelope reproduces its source curve within ~0.06 (out of a 0–1 range) at the worst sample point.
- Chunks' runtime asmdef (`Runtime/Chunks/Chunks.asmdef`) now references `com.Lautaro-Arino.Laubrary.ZuiRuntime` for `ZUIEnvelopePoint`/`ZUIEnvelopeEvaluator`.
- Follow-up (out of scope here): `ZuiReflect.cs` still falls back to `Z.Curve` when reflecting a bare `AnimationCurve` field — that's the generic reflection drawer used by every tool, not Chunks-specific, and was left alone per this task's scope.

### T-0252 — Debris Scatter: live Zoe-sprite sampling + project pixel scale default
- **A Chunks Debris Scatter can now sample from a Zoe's own live sprite, not just an authored asset.** `ChunkModuleContext` carries a new `SampleSourceOverride` slot alongside its existing `Palette` one; `SpawnChunkFx` and `PyreChunksFx` (the two Zoe-event effects that spawn Chunks) both forward the Zoe's CURRENT on-screen sprite as this override every time they fire, sharing one implementation (`Runtime/ZoetropePyre/ZoeLiveSampler.cs`) with the colour-palette sampling that already existed for `SpawnChunkFx`. A Sampled-visual Debris Scatter prefers this live override when present and falls back to its own authored `sampleSource` field otherwise — a standalone burst with no Zoe context (no override supplied) behaves exactly as before this existed.
- **Floating Disc's Hit debris now samples the disc's own current sprite and colours**, live, every hit — previously its `PyreChunksFx` reaction sampled neither (a pre-existing gap; only `SpawnChunkFx` had colour sampling).
- **New live-sample preview in the Zoetrope window.** A Spawn Chunks / Pyre + Chunks effect row on a Zoe reaction now shows a foldable "Live sample preview": the frame standing in for the Zoe's current sprite, the palette it would tint debris with, and (when the recipe has a Sampled-visual Debris Scatter) a few example cut pieces — so an author can see what will actually get sampled at runtime instead of guessing.
- **Debris pixel size now defaults to the project's Pixel Scale setting, not a hardcoded 32.** `DebrisScatter` gained `useProjectPixelScale` (on by default) and an explicit `pixelsPerUnitOverride` field, mirroring `PixelScaleCamera`'s own override-toggle shape; a legacy authored `pixelsPerUnit` value migrates onto the override with `useProjectPixelScale` turned off, so already-tuned recipes are unaffected. The Chunks window's Debris Scatter card shows the toggle plus the live effective value.
- **Debris piece size (`sizeMin`/`sizeMax`) defaults bumped** from 0.08–0.18 to 0.16–0.35 world units — at the project's PPU-16 scale the old range was 1.3–2.9 SCREEN pixels (sub-pixel), which read as fine noise rather than blocky pixel-art shrapnel; the new range was matched by eye against ProtoGuy's own sprite density in Play mode. Freely author-tunable either way. Floating Disc's private Hit debris asset was updated to the new size range as the real-world test case (its pixel-scale fields already picked up the new project-setting default automatically via the migration above).

### T-0251 — Screen-edge visualiser + BackSplash runtime backdrop
- **Screen-edge visualiser** (`Runtime/PixelScale/ScreenEdgeVisualizer.cs`) — a generic, reusable helper (originally scoped as Life Manager task T-0007) that draws where the game screen's edges fall, in world units, derived entirely from `PixelScaleProjectSettings`: the core rectangle at the project's target resolution (320x200 by default), and an outer rectangle showing the extra area a larger display (a configurable `outerResolution`, default 1920x1080) reveals around that core, computed via the project's own integer-upscale rule so it stays consistent with what a real pixel-perfect camera renders. One on/off toggle, nothing else configurable. Draws via Gizmos in the editor and via runtime `LineRenderer`s during Play (visible in the Game view too), matching the debug-line pattern T-0247's `FireDirectionVisualizer` established. Wired onto ProtoGuyDemo's Main Camera.
- **BackSplash runtime backdrop** (`Runtime/BackSplash/BackSplashBackdrop.cs`) — the runtime counterpart the package didn't have yet (only editor-side IMGUI preview painting existed). Renders a `BackSplash` asset as a live Play-mode background: sets the target camera's clear colour from `cameraColor` and positions/scales a `SpriteRenderer` from `image`/`imageZoom`/`imagePos`, reusing the asset's own fields with no parallel data model. ProtoGuyDemo now has a `ProtoGuyDemo Background.asset` BackSplash using `Assets/BackSplash/Source/Background22.png` (imported as a Sprite), and a debug-only OnGUI zoom slider (`Assets/Demos/ProtoGuyDemo/BackSplashZoomSlider.cs`, same plain-game-code pattern as `FireDirectionVisualizer`) for tuning `imageZoom` live by eye.

### T-0250 — Private (embedded) Chunks reference
- **A Zoe's Chunks reaction row can now be Public or Private.** Public is exactly today's behaviour — pick a shared Chunks asset from the library. Private embeds a copy of the Chunks recipe INSIDE the Zoe's own asset file, so it never clutters the shared Chunks browser — nothing else can pick it, because nothing else can see it. A Private row shows the embedded asset's name and an Edit button (its only entry point, since it isn't browsable) that opens the normal Chunks window on that exact instance.
- **Switching modes never destroys data.** Turning a row Private for the first time creates the embedded copy from whatever was already assigned (if anything), so authored tuning survives the switch; turning it back to Public just changes what the row shows — it does not delete or clear the embedded asset, so nothing is silently lost either way.
- **Floating Disc's death debris ("Floating Disc Blowup") is now Private** on `Assets/Zoetrope/Floating Disc.asset` — it was bespoke to this one Zoe and had no reason to sit in the shared browser. The original shared asset is untouched (the ChunksDemo scene still uses it); the Zoe now points at its own embedded copy. Its hit-reaction spark burst ("Sparks") stays Public — that one is a generic, reusable effect.
- **Fixes (AssetKit).** `AssetLibrary<T>.Enumerate` / `AssetLibraryUntyped.Enumerate` — the shared read paths behind every LauAsset browser/picker — now exclude sub-assets. `AssetDatabase.LoadAssetAtPath<T>` falls back to a matching sub-asset when the file's main asset is a different type, so without this an asset meant to be embedded-and-hidden would still surface everywhere. `ZuiAssetWindow<T>`'s Duplicate/Rename/Delete toolbar buttons (and its Tags section) are now hidden for a sub-asset too — those actions operate on the asset's FILE, which a sub-asset shares with whatever owns it.

### T-0245 — Pyre anchor
- **A Pyre can carry an optional anchor.** Off by default, so every existing asset keeps its implicit origin (the canvas centre). Turned on in the Pyre window's Canvas section, the anchor is either a Position (the Pyre's base / start / centre) or a Vector (that point plus the direction the art faces), placed by clicking the preview and dragging the dot or the arrowhead — the same marker Launimator's Vector meta-layers use, now one shared implementation (`ZuiVectorMarker`) rather than two. The anchor does nothing by itself: it is the author telling placing systems how to treat the Pyre. Anchor and origin are deliberately ONE concept — when an anchor is set it IS the origin a placing system pins to the spawn point.
- **Every Pyre-spawning Zoe effect and the weapon muzzle slot honour it.** A spawned blast lands its anchor (not its centre) on the resolved point, and "Face event direction" rotates a Vector anchor's direction along the event instead of assuming the art faces +X. A Pyre without an anchor is placed exactly as before.

### T-0239 — Muzzle flash follows the muzzle (rework 2)
- **A followed effect now tracks its whole pose, not just its position.** An effect card's Follow used to re-sample only the point it spawned at, so a muzzle flash slid along with the barrel while still pointing the way the shot originally left. It now re-asks the card's own Rotate setting every frame as well, so ProtoGuy's flash rides the painted Muzzle point AND keeps pointing where the gun points — and re-mirrors — while the character walks or turns mid-flash. A Random direction is still rolled once at spawn and held, so it scatters rather than spins.
- **A weapon's aim reaches an effect as a live answer, not a snapshot.** The Fire event carries an ongoing direction source alongside the direction it was raised with; anything that re-asks gets where the gun points now, while a hit or a death (which have no ongoing source) behave exactly as before.
- **Fixes.** A pooled blast no longer accumulates a follower per reuse — the follow is re-armed on the existing component and cleared when the pool deactivates the instance. The Zoe's animated view and body-part lookup are now checked for real liveness before being re-read, so an effect still playing when its character is destroyed stops where it is instead of throwing every frame.

### T-0239 — Directional muzzle flash (rework)
- **ProtoGuy fires ONE muzzle flash again.** Two flashes had always been authored on top of each other — the gun's own muzzle slot and the Zoe Fire event's Pyre row, both at the painted Muzzle point; the first attempt moved only one of them, which is what pulled the pair apart. The gun's slot is now empty and the Fire event's row is the one flash: at the painted Muzzle meta point, turned with the shot, mirrored for a left-facing shot.
- **Rotation and mirroring are resolved together and mean the same thing on both spawn paths.** An effect card's rotation now defaults to None (nothing authored before it existed rotates); with "Mirror left" on, a result that points left shows the mirrored visual at a small angle rather than the right-facing art rotated 180° and drawn upside down — the effect compensates the angle through its Pyre anchor. A weapon's own muzzle slot plays oriented the same way (`ICombatFx.PlayOriented`), so whichever of the two owns the flash points it correctly.
- **Zoe window.** Rotation controls read for the current mode (Offset° / Angle° as sliders, a per-mode "Mirror left" tooltip), and an effect whose Pyre has a Vector anchor says so beside the picker. A duplicated test file was removed and a data test now pins ProtoGuy's Fire to exactly one blast.

### T-0242
- **ProtoGuy demo / Zoetrope.** The Floating Disc's declared hit reaction now combines its directional Pyre impact with a Sparks Chunks emission, while its declared death continues through the authored Floating Disc Blowup recipe: a multi-Pyre fracture burst with the recipe's small fire trails. The debris recipe now uses the shared 16 PPU world scale, and the demo keeps four lower-health flyers in a tighter, faster respawn loop so the hit and kill reads are encountered naturally in play.

### Added
- **Zoetrope — directional event effects.** Named events now carry a normalized general direction, and each event effect can anchor to a declared body part with a local offset, choose no/event-facing/fixed rotation with an art offset, and mirror compatible Pyre flashes with body facing. ProtoGuy's Fire flash uses the Upper part and the shot direction.

### Fixed
- **Zoetrope / SpriteFx.** Body SpriteFx now treats a composite Zoe as its declared body-part renderer set rather than selecting the first descendant renderer, so a body flash, tint or relight reaches the whole figure without affecting equipped or transient child FX. Body SpriteFx cards can optionally restrict themselves to one declared part; single-sprite Zoes preserve their existing fallback.

### Added
- **Pixel Scale** (`Runtime/PixelScale/`, asmdef `com.Lautaro-Arino.Laubrary.PixelScale`) — a host-project asset establishes the pixel-art tile scale, low-resolution world canvas, human-height reference and display reference once. `PixelScale` derives camera size and integer preview/display scale from it, while `PixelScaleCamera` makes an orthographic world camera follow those values by default or explicitly override them in one scene. It leaves UI cameras and canvases alone, and configures an already-installed Pixel Perfect Camera without adding a render-pipeline dependency.
- **Mirage — optional Game-view camera navigation (T-0236).** Its HUD now has two switches that are both off by default: **Mouse camera** makes middle-drag pan and the wheel zoom the preview camera, while **Keyboard camera** lets WASD or arrow keys pan it. Previewable placement and dragging keep their existing left-mouse controls, and the opt-in switches leave player input alone unless an author deliberately turns them on.
- **DotGen** (`Runtime/DotGen/`, asmdef `com.Lautaro-Arino.Laubrary.DotGen`, namespace `Laubrary.DotGen`) — the generative engine behind a new composition tool: a recursive system for making pictures out of areas and dots, with no built-in idea of what the picture is of. A **generator** owns an area; its **placement** fills that area with dots (a lattice, a packed row of variable-width boxes, or rings of points) and usually gives each dot the cell around it; **selectors** score those dots from 0 to 1 (distance from the edge, a directional ramp or wave, a deterministic random mask) without changing anything; ordered **mutators** move or remove them through a selector (nudge, warp toward centres or parallel lines, cull); ordered **drawers** paint areas or cells, filled with a `ZuiFill` so a fill can be a flat colour, a gradient at any angle, or a random pick from a list. Every surviving dot can then carry a whole child generator, sized either to its parent's area or to the exact cell around it, and never clipped to either — which is how one row of boxes grows up through the row behind it, and how a grid of windows lands on each box, without either concept existing in the tool. The whole pipeline is deterministic: one seed and the document decide every draw, so the same asset renders the same frame on any machine, and the renderer is a plain CPU rasteriser (background, guide grid, drawers, frame, dot markers with their glow) that produces both the preview and the PNG export from one path — process overlays are not part of it, so an export can never contain one. The documented demonstration composition evaluates to 308 visible dots across 32 generator areas in about 3.5 ms at 512 px.
- **Lattice** (`Runtime/Lattice/`, asmdef `com.Lautaro-Arino.Laubrary.Lattice`, namespace `Laubrary.Lattice`, zero dependencies) — a square lattice of cells with an editable edge set ("dots and streets"), plus everything a game needs to move along it and reveal it. Extracted from the BurnOutCity POC where it was hardened. `LatticeGraph` owns the edges, an O(1) `EdgeFrom(cell, dir)` index, and the cell↔world mapping (`CellWorld`, `NearestCell`, XZ or XY plane, `Origin`). `LatticeEdge` carries per-chunk coverage: every edge is split into `ChunkCount` pieces (`CellSize / ChunkWorldSize`, a constructor parameter) that flip independently, so partial reveal works at any resolution. `LatticeGen` bakes a city from a seed: full lattice, void clusters, 5×5 rings, stepped diagonal spines with pruning, then a union-find repair so the result is always one connected component (`IsConnected()` verifies it). `LatticeWalker` is headless, plain-C# movement clamped to the edges — input is a stick vector, the walker picks the nearest cardinal with a real edge, a mid-edge turn walks back to the vertex at normal speed instead of snapping — so it is unit-testable without Play mode. `LatticeSweep` covers chunks from a moving point's swept path (previous → current position) and fires `EdgeTouched` / `ChunkCovered` / `EdgeCovered`; being purely geometric it cannot bleed reveal onto edges the path never entered. Lattice itself never renders.
- **LatticeLines** (`Runtime/LatticeLines/`, asmdef `com.Lautaro-Arino.Laubrary.LatticeLines`, depends on Lattice only) — the vector renderer: `LatticeLineView`, one LineRenderer per edge coloured by material swap from a consumer-supplied integer state (`UniformState(edge)` or -1 for per-chunk, then `ChunkState(edge, i)`), pooled run segments for partially-covered edges, and a change cache so unchanged edges cost no property writes.
- **LatticePixels** (`Runtime/LatticePixels/`, asmdef `com.Lautaro-Arino.Laubrary.LatticePixels`, depends on Lattice only) — the pixel-art renderer. `LatticePixelCanvas` is the plain-C# truth: a world-aligned texel grid at a chosen pixels-per-unit, roads rasterised once as bands `RoadWidthPx` wide, a reveal mask painted by stamping a brush (any alpha silhouette, e.g. the player's sprite) along the mover's swept path (`StampSweep` sub-steps by half the brush so fast frames leave no gaps), and edge completion decided by "every centreline texel painted" (`EdgeCovered`, plus `RoadPixelRevealed` per newly painted road texel). `LatticePixelView` uploads it: R8 road and reveal masks (only the dirty rect per frame via `Graphics.CopyTexture`), a quad over the lattice, and `Resources/LatticePixels.shader` (URP), which colours each road texel from reveal × an optional per-pixel vision cone (`SetVision(pos, dir, halfAngle, range, omniRadius)`) using four consumer colours. Vision has an occluded mode too (`SetVisionOccluded`): the canvas casts `ShadowRays` rays across the cone through the road mask (`CastShadowMap`), anything that is not road blocks them, and the shader looks each pixel's angle up in the resulting 1D shadow map, so the cone only reaches along the street being looked down; `IsClearPath(a, b)` gives consumers the same test for their own objects. The vision test itself lives in `Resources/LatticeVision.hlsl` and is driven by GLOBAL shader parameters published by the static `LatticeVision` class (`SetGraph`, `SetCone`, `SetOccluded`, `Clear`), so any material can include it and get the identical per-pixel answer the roads get; `Resources/LatticePixelsSprite.shader` is the first: a sprite whose in-sight pixels draw normally and whose out-of-sight pixels draw at `_HiddenAlpha` (0 = clipped away, a small value = a ghost), for enemies and props that must only show the part of themselves that is actually in view. Both renderers consume the same graph, so a game can switch between pixel and vector roads with no change to its data or rules.

- **Double-click any dial to put it back the way it shipped (T-0227).** Every slider drawn from an object's own fields — a Pyre modifier or form, a SpriteFx effect, a Chunks card, a Lathe module, a rule's parameters — now restores the value the author wrote into the type when you double-click it, and says which number that is in its own hover text rather than the word "default". It is one undo step and one re-render, exactly like letting go of a drag, so a dial that has been pushed somewhere unrecoverable is one gesture away from being back where it started. Sliders whose owner is a component or asset (rather than a piece of authored data inside one) are unchanged.
- **A nine-point anchor picker for any tool that attaches a box by one of its corners (T-0227).** `Z.AnchorGrid` — a 3×3 grid where the cell you press IS the point, with the anchor's name spelled out beside it, instead of a nine-entry list that makes you translate "Bottom right" into a position every time you read it.
- **DotGen has a window (T-0228).** `Laubrary/DotGen` opens a document the way Pyre opens a blast: browse or make one and it starts as a working composition rather than an empty frame, with the picture beside the dials and a backdrop behind it so a design can be judged against the surface it will sit on. The Frame section owns the seed, the resolution, the colours drawn behind and around the composition, and the two things you do to a whole document — draw a new seed, or write it out as a PNG at the size shown. The Hierarchy is the tree of generators: each row says what its placement is and how many dots it is currently making, and the row of commands beside it adds a child (sized for what its parent hands out), reorders it among its siblings, copies it with everything below it, or removes it — every one of them undoable, and none of them available on the root, which cannot be moved, copied or deleted. The Generator section is the selected generator's own area: its shape, its size, which of its nine points sits on the thing it is attached to, and — for a child — how many of its parent's dots it appears on. The picture zooms 25–800% to the cursor, pans by dragging, and fits again on a double-click or `0`.
- **DotGen's four module sections are now authorable (T-0229).** Placement, Selectors, Mutators and Drawers each hold the selected generator's own cards. Placement is a choice of method and the dials of whichever one is chosen — and each method keeps its own dials, so trying rings on a lattice you spent ten minutes on and changing your mind gives the lattice back exactly as it was. A selector, a mutator or a drawer is added from a button that names what it adds, renamed in place, switched off without losing a single setting (its card says so and puts its dials away), dragged by its grip into a different position where position is what decides the result, and removed — every one of those undoable. A mutator and a cell-painting drawer point at a selector by picking its name from the ones this generator declares, never by typing anything, the list relabels itself the moment a selector is renamed, and deleting a selector quietly puts everything that referenced it back on All dots rather than leaving a reference to something that no longer exists. A Fill drawer painting from a list of fills gets that list in order, with a flat colour or a gradient added to the end on one press and the last remaining entry protected from removal. Hovering any card draws that module's process overlays on the picture and outlines the card in the same colour, so the overlay and the card that owns it read as one thing; clicking a card's header makes it the sticky overlay target and folds it away, while its own controls stay usable without folding anything.
- **DotGen's preview now draws the process behind the picture, not just the picture (T-0230).** Every generator area, placement lattice/rings/box row, selector weight, mutator's before-and-after, and drawer's paint target from POC §13 now has its own overlay on the frame, in the generator's own colour where one applies — hovering a card shows only that module's overlay by default, the Frame section's Gizmos control switches to the selected module, everything the selected generator has, or none at all, and every category carries the same detail cap the reference does so a dense composition never draws more gizmos than the picture can read. Nothing here touches the renderer or the export, which is exactly the point: a gizmo can show what a mutator or a drawer did without ever being part of what either of them actually painted.
- **A DotGen picture is no longer drawn upside down (T-0228).** The generator pipeline measures y downward from the frame's top edge, which is what makes an area anchored Bottom grow upward — but the rasterised frame was handed to the texture and to the PNG bottom row first, so every composition came out mirrored and every anchor meant its opposite. Preview and export both come out the right way up now.
- **DotGen ships with two demonstrations, both authored through the window itself (T-0231).** `Assets/Demos/DotGenDemo/DotGen Demo.asset` is the tool's own reference composition — a lattice carrying two child generators, 308 visible dots across 32 evaluated areas — created with nothing more than the window's own New button. `Three Rows of Buildings.asset` reproduces the tool's worked example end to end, one real control at a time: a one-column, three-row Root Area; a Box Row generator standing each box on its own bottom edge and letting it grow past its row into whatever is behind it, which is how a nearer row's tall buildings climb up through the row standing behind them with no special case anywhere in the renderer; a Fill drawer painting every box; and a child Grid attached to each box's own cell, narrowed by a Margin selector to a safe interior and painted by a second Fill drawer — window panes that never touch a wall edge. Both documents export straight to PNG from the window, and `Laubrary Dev/Copy Demos to Package` carries them into `Samples~/Demos/DotGenDemo/` alongside every other tool's demo.
- **A Pyre Blast can tint and fade what it spawns, per instance (T-0222).** One effect asset, several looks: the blast's `Tint` multiplies onto every renderer of what it spawns, and an `Alpha` range gives each individual blast of a pattern its own opacity, drawn from the same seeded sequence the angle and the size come from — so a ring of five can come out as five differently faded blasts of one Pyre without authoring five assets. Colour is per-USE, deliberately: it lives on the recipe's blast, not on the effect, so the same Pyre picked into three recipes still looks like itself in the other two. Existing recipes are untouched (white, fully solid, and no extra draw off the sequence). The editor preview's discs take the tint and each instance's own alpha, so the dials change the picture rather than only the burst.

### Fixed
- **A reflected choice of three or fewer options now looks like the ones written by hand (T-0232).** Every window that draws an object's own fields — Pyre, Chunks, SpriteFx, Lathe, DotGen — used to render a short enum as a wrapping row of mini radios while the same window's hand-written choices used the joined segmented control, so two identical kinds of choice sat side by side looking like two different controls. Short sets are now segmented everywhere; longer ones still wrap, because a joined row of six would run off the pane.
- **Double-clicking a dial whose default depends on where it is used now restores the right number (T-0232).** A reflected slider resets to the value its type was written with, which is correct until one type serves two roles — a DotGen lattice is 8×7 on a root generator and 4×4 on a child, and the reset offered 4 in both. A dial's owner can now answer the question itself, so the reset and the creation can never give different answers.
- **A DotGen card's name no longer loses its last letter (T-0232).** The rename field in a card header was sharing the row's spare width with an empty spacer, which left it about one character short of an ordinary default name: "Edge margin" read as "Edge margi". The name takes the width it needs now.
- **Hovering a DotGen generator in the Hierarchy shows you where it is (T-0232).** Hovered gizmos followed the module cards only, so the one question a tree row raises — "where does THAT one sit?" — had no answer without first selecting it and losing your place. Hovering a row now outlines that generator's areas on the picture, whichever generator you are actually editing.
- **The DotGen buildings demonstration reads as buildings (T-0232).** Its window panes were painted the same colour as the walls, so the composition the tool exists to demonstrate showed as plain grey boxes; the windows are lit now, and the dot markers that sat on top of the paint are off — which is also the clearest demonstration that dots still drive children and drawers with their markers hidden.
- **Smaller DotGen window drift (T-0232).** Clicking a generator no longer blanks every row's dot count until the picture next redraws; the resolution dial shares the seed's row instead of taking one of its own; the placement card no longer repeats the method name that is written directly above it; the two explanatory captions in the Generator block moved into the tooltips of the controls they were explaining; and the left pane no longer draws a horizontal scrollbar it can never use.
- **A pooled Pyre blast player no longer keeps the last user's colour (T-0222).** Players are pooled and handed on, and nothing put the renderer's colour back — so once anything recoloured one (a tinted Chunks blast), the next effect to borrow that instance came out in the previous shot's colour. A play now always starts from the frames' own colours.
- **A pattern's FIRST blast no longer gets a one-frame head start on the rest (T-0216).** In a Pyre Blast laid out as a Line or a Ring, the point that goes off immediately was already moving by the end of the frame it was born in, while every staggered point started clean — so it landed off the placement its own formation had resolved, by an amount that grew with how late you looked (about half a ring spacing when it was first measured). It was not a placement error: the blast was spawned in exactly the right place and then moved before anything could see it, and whether that happened at all depended on Unity's execution order for a component added mid-frame. Flight now always starts on the frame after the hand-over, so every point of a pattern flies from where the recipe says it does — and the editor preview, which has always drawn it that way, now agrees with the burst point for point.
- **Rest on floor now shows in the Chunks preview (T-0216).** The preview bounced debris off the floor but ignored what happens once a piece settles, so switching Rest on floor off left a floor full of debris in the picture that the real burst clears within a beat.
- **Picking a new asset now updates what the picker says (T-0216).** Every Chunk Spec / Pyre / animation / trail-source picker in the Chunks window and in the Follow Emitter inspector kept showing the OLD asset's name after a pick, until something else happened to rebuild it.
- **Debris Scatter no longer shows a Spin dial that does nothing.** A tumbling sampled cut takes its rate from Tumble speed, and Spin is not read at all in that mode.
- **The Follow Emitter's Sorting Order field no longer runs off the edge of the inspector**, taking a horizontal scrollbar with it.

### Changed
- **One drag of a ZUI slider, range slider, pad or scrub field is now ONE undo step (T-0216).** It used to record a step per pointer move, so Ctrl+Z after a two-second drag walked back through the drag frame by frame instead of undoing it. The controls know where a gesture starts and ends, so the fix lives in them and every tool gets it without changing a call site.
- **The Chunks preview now decides what is in FRONT the same way the burst does (T-0216).** It used to draw anything without a named layer slot behind everything slotted; the burst does the opposite, because an unslotted output takes the emitter's own draw order (500 by default) plus its place in the recipe, which is a bigger number than any slot's. The preview now asks the runtime's own resolver, so a composition mixing slotted and unslotted producers previews the depth it will actually have. The slot picker's wording and the four producers' Layer tooltips said the old thing and now say the true one.
- **The Chunks preview frames the composition instead of its furthest particle (T-0216).** One fragment still travelling at the end of the clock used to set the zoom for the whole run, so the first half-second showed as a speck in the middle. The stage now fits about nine tenths of what is on screen at the clock's midpoint, then widens — never narrows — to keep the origin and every authored formation point inside. A producer's aim arrow is capped to the stage rather than allowed to push the framing out.
- **Fragment Fracture's Pieces is a slider over the range the cutter actually honours (2–12), not an open number (T-0216).** The field accepted 1, or 40, and the cut silently gave you 2 or 12. Gravity (Debris Scatter, Fragment Fracture, Palette Splash) and Debris Scatter's Upward bias are sliders too, matching what Trajectory already did with the same two values.
- **Every capability card can be given a name of its own (T-0216).** A recipe holding three Pyre Blasts is unreadable until they are "Flash", "Ring" and "Aftershock". A card has carried an optional name since the capability stack landed — the upgrade even writes one, from an old blast group's label — but nothing in the window could set or clear it. Empty still means the kind's own name.
- **A Debris Scatter's Pixels/unit says what it does.** It sets the generated chunk sprite's RESOLUTION; how big a chunk is on screen is the Size dial's business and always was. The tooltip claimed it decided a sampled cut's world size, which it does not.
- **⚠️ Chunks is now an authored STACK of composable capabilities, with a window and a moving preview to match, and every existing recipe is upgraded once, on disk (T-0208–T-0215). Read this before syncing — it rewrites your `.asset` files.** A recipe used to be one debris burst plus seven optional modules that were either on or off, could only ever appear once each, and were scheduled by a timeline that addressed them by name. It is now an ordered list of capabilities — Debris Scatter, Fragment Fracture, Palette Splash, Pyre Blast, Trajectory, Trail, Hits, Layer Plan, Cues — of which a recipe may hold any number, in any order, each with its own dials and its own **absolute** moment on one shared clock. That last word is the behavioural change: a delay used to be looked up per named slot, so only the slots that had names could be timed at all, and two blasts could not sit at different moments unless they happened to be different modules. Three consequences worth checking after the upgrade: (1) **The recipe's own `Direction` stays recipe-level** — it is the composition's aim, and each producer still chooses only how wide its cone is; (2) **anything you did not put in a named layer slot now draws in the order you authored it in** rather than every piece landing on the emitter's one number — a recipe with several producers and no Layer Plan may therefore change which piece is in front; (3) **seeded randomness resolves differently.** Every roll now comes off one generator seeded from the recipe's own seed rather than off `System.Random`, so an authored seed still reproduces exactly, forever, on every platform — but the arrangement it reproduces is not the one the old seed gave. An unseeded recipe is unaffected (it still rerolls every play). The upgrade runs automatically the first time each recipe is loaded and is written back through the normal asset pipeline, so **expect your ChunkSpec assets to show up as modified on the first editor launch after syncing**; the old fields are still on the asset, read only by that upgrade, and come out one release from now. Public burst APIs (`Chunks.Burst`, `ChunkEmitter.Burst`, `ChunkEmitter.SpawnBurst`) are unchanged in every signature and behaviour.
  The window is rebuilt around the stack rather than a fixed set of panels: one card per capability — fold it, switch it off, move it up or down, remove it, or press `Add capability…` for one of the nine kinds, with anything that has nothing yet to act on greyed and saying why. All nine cards carry real dials (Debris Scatter's Visual switch between Squares/Sprites/Sampled/Animated showing only its own mode's fields; Fragment Fracture and Palette Splash's animated-or-plain sources; Pyre Blast's single-or-pool source and Single/Line/Ring pattern; Trajectory/Trail/Hits each leading with a Target picker for which producer they ride; Layer Plan's ordered named depth slots, with a rename or removal rewriting every producer that pointed at the old slot inside the same undo step; Cues' declared-Code-or-picked-Zound moments). A Timing section (and each card's own Delay dial) appears only once the recipe actually has more than one timed capability. The preview stage is no longer a static backdrop: it draws the recipe as a schematic that MOVES at wall-clock speed on the recipe's own real arcs — gravity, drag, upward kick, floor bounce — deterministically per seed, with Play/Loop/Replay, the scrub handle, the Timing playhead and a mid-play dial edit all staying in sync and nothing ever left stale or blank between frames. The two non-window inspectors (`ChunkFollowEmitterEditor`, `ChunkSpecEditor`) are ZUI now, `ChunkSpecEditor` gaining a read-only Capabilities summary and an `Open in Chunks Window` doorway. The `ChunksDemo` scene's four recipes were re-authored through the window onto the new model — a plain scatter, a sampled/tumbling scatter, a fracture-and-splash composed on two layers, and a Pyre ring pattern with a trajectory and a cue — and the `Preview in Mirage` handoff was verified against a real running burst, not just the schematic.
- **⚠️ Asking a Zoe to show a named state is now trustworthy, and four things about it changed at once (T-0092). Read this before syncing — it is the only entry here that can alter what an existing, untouched asset does at runtime.** (1) **State names are matched ignoring case.** `Zoe.EventNamed` was the only authored name in the whole module still compared ordinally — cues, meta-layer ids, clip names and `FxEntry` override names all ignored case already — so `"fire"` from game code missed a state declared as `"Fire"` while resolving perfectly as an override name on the same request. This is strictly widening: every name that matched before still matches, and only previously-failing near-misses start matching. The Zoe window's duplicate badge, its new-event naming and its cue Raise picker were all moved to the same rule in the same change, so the editor cannot bless a pair the runtime treats as one name. (2) **A missed name is now LOUD.** `ReactionFxPlayer.Raise` warns — once per name per character, with the character as the log context — and the message LISTS what the character does declare, which is the fact that turns "no such state" from a guess between a typo, a rename and the wrong character into an answer. Measured before the change: raising an undeclared name on ProtoGuy produced no warning, no refusal signal and no checked return value. **Expect this to surface latent bugs in existing projects on first run** — that is the point, but it will look like new console spam. A new `TryRaise` is the silent form, for a name offered on spec rather than chosen. (3) **A state request carries position and facing.** A raise takes a `ReactionRequest` (see Added), and the weapon fire path fills it with the LIVE muzzle position and the shot's resolved aim. **Pre-sync check:** an `FxEntry` on a game-raised state left on the default `placement: HitPosition` moves from the character's anchor to the muzzle, and one left on the default `direction: HitDirection` goes from omni-directional to aimed along the shot — grep each project's Zoe assets for `placement: 0` / `direction: 0` inside an `events:` block and eyeball those effects. Hit and death are byte-identical: `ReactionRequest.From` reproduces the old `point != zero` fallback exactly. (4) **`stunSeconds` is now obeyed, from whichever reaction actually played.** It was authorable on every reaction and read in exactly one place — `ZoeSpawner` froze `hit.stunSeconds` onto `ZoeState` at spawn — so a stun on a death or a named state did nothing, and editing a live character's hit stun did nothing either. That spawn-time copy is gone; `ReactionFxPlayer` applies the value live. **Pre-sync check:** grep each project's Zoe assets for `stunSeconds:` greater than zero and confirm every one was intended, because none of them has ever been vetted. Note what a stun actually gates today: `ZoeState.CanAct` is consulted by the two ANIMATORS and by nothing else, so a stun freezes the walk/idle cycle and does not stop the character moving or firing — a 0.5 s stun on a full-auto weapon's fire state leaves it permanently frozen in its idle pose. The `stunSeconds` tooltip now says exactly that instead of promising it "stops moving and acting".
- **A multi-projectile shot now asks for its fire state ONCE, not once per projectile (T-0093).** `ProjectileWeapon.FireInternal` raises `Fired` for every projectile, so a three-pellet shotgun asked its character for the fire state three times in one frame — the arbiter refused two of the three clips while all three still fired the reaction's Immediate effects. This reads as a fix, but it IS a visible difference for anyone who tuned an effect against the tripled version. Deliberately scoped to the state raise only: the muzzle VFX and the fire zound are still per-projectile, and de-duplicating those is a separate change nobody asked for.
- **`PlayLauminationEffect` says plainly that it plays a bare clip.** Its tooltip and doc comment now state that it carries no duration, no body flash and no effect list, and that raising a named state is the way to get those. (It already routed through the `AnimationArbiter` rather than the view; that is unchanged.)

### Added
- **ZUI can draw a clock whose parts overlap, and a divider you dragged too far can be put back (T-0209).** `Z.Lanes` is a new multi-lane clock: N named bands, each placed by its own ABSOLUTE start and end on ONE shared ruler, with named instants and a single draggable playhead. `Z.Timeline` remains what it always was — a strip of CONSECUTIVE bands — which means an absolute "starts at 0.15 s" dial drawn on it silently reads as "0.15 s after the previous one ended", so any tool whose parts run together (a chunk recipe's sparks and its debris) needs the new control instead. Three of its behaviours are corrections to measured defects rather than decoration: the ruler's length is set by the HOST over every band including the switched-off ones (otherwise disabling one rescales the ruler and slides the rest sideways), a switched-off band keeps its lane drawn dim (removing it changes the control's height and moves what sits above), and the first and last ruler numbers are pulled inside the bar (centred on their own tick, `0.00s` renders as `.00s` against the edge). Two smaller fixes ride along: a ruler number the playhead's readout covers is now **dropped out of the lane** in `Z.Timeline` too, rather than left in the tree invisible and still answering hit tests from underneath; and **double-clicking a `Z.Split` divider resets the pane to the width the tool asks for and forgets the saved one** — because that width persists, a cold open was never a first run, and a pane dragged to an extreme had no route back for any Laubrary tool.
- **The palette's honesty / bypass-resistance surface (T-0096).** A small set of visibility tools so nobody — including an AI writing game code — quietly starts making a character show something without going through its declared list. `ZoeReactionTelemetry.Requested` (Runtime, no Editor reference — same shape as `ChunkTimelineEvents.HasListeners`) reports every attempt to raise a name against a character's palette, matched or not; `ZoePaletteUsageLog` (Editor, `[InitializeOnLoad]`) persists what it hears to a Library-folder cache across domain reloads and editor restarts. Zoe window: every custom event row now shows a usage chip ("● requested N×" / "○ never requested"), a ▶ Preview button (spawns a throwaway character via `ZoeSpawner.SpawnCharacter` — the same path Mirage's own edit-mode preview uses — and fires the reaction for real through `ReactionFxPlayer`/`Health`, no Play mode needed), and a copy-name button (for pasting into a hand-typed `Raise("...")` call, per the project's "a name is typed once where declared" rule); the built-in Hit/Death rows get the same Preview button. Deleting an event now warns (`EditorUtility.DisplayDialog`) if a cue on the same character still raises it or if it has ever been observed requested. New `Laubrary/Zoetrope/Palette Health` window (one more item in the existing Zoetrope submenu) aggregates two project-wide checks: **names requested that no character declares** (the collected form of `ReactionFxPlayer.WarnNoSuchState`'s existing per-instance console warning), and the REVERSE check per ZOE_PALETTE_TAKE.md's "bypass question" — **a character showing something that did NOT come from its declared palette** — a heuristic text scan (`ZoePaletteBypassAudit`) for direct `PyreBlastPool.Get`/`ChunkPool.Get`/`.PlayFollowable`/`.AddComponent<SpriteFxFilter>`/`.PlayClip` calls outside Laubrary's own package, always shown as a review prompt rather than a verdict. `Zoe.DescribeDeclaredStates()` prints a character's whole declared list as plain text, for writing gameplay code away from the editor. CLAUDE.md's Zoe palette section now names these tools explicitly, next to the rule they exist to help enforce.
- **The palette model: role chip, body-part targeting, unified hurt/death presentation, default+override effects (T-0095).** The actual new feature the whole modularity effort was building toward. Four pieces, all additive — every already-authored Zoe asset loads and behaves exactly as before, gaining only new optional settings. (1) **Role chip** — `NamedReaction.role` (`ReactionRole.None`/`Hurt`/`Death`), a three-way tag saying whether a custom event is a legal answer to Laubrary's two built-in questions. Defaults to `None`. The built-in Hit/Death rows get the equivalent chip DRAWN in the Zoe window (a `[HURT]`/`[DEATH]` label beside their section header) rather than stored, so nothing already authored is touched or restructured. (2) **Body-part targeting** — `ReactionFx.targetPart`: empty (the default) plays whole-body exactly as every reaction always has; naming a composite part (e.g. "Legs") confines the reaction to that one part, so a character can show more than one thing at once (walking legs under a firing upper body). Wired through `IArbitratedView.PlayClipArbitrated`'s new optional `targetPart` parameter, `AnimationArbiter.Play`/`Replay`, and `CompositeAnimatedView.Fan`'s participant filter — a name matching no known part plays on nothing (the same "refused" outcome an unknown clip name already has), never silently falls back to the whole body. Zoe window: a "Target Part" dropdown appears on every reaction card, sourced from the same part list `Zoe.weapons`' "Attach To Part" already uses, and only shows at all on a composite Zoe. (3) **Ask, don't default** — the new optional `IReactionLookAnswerer` interface (`HurtLookFor`/`DeathLookFor(in DamageInfo)`), discovered via `GetComponentInParent` exactly like `IFireStateSource`. With no such component, or one that answers null/empty, a character's Hit/Death plays EXACTLY as it always has — this is the honest "nobody answered yet" fallback (ZOE_PALETTE_TAKE.md), not a hidden default-picker: nothing here ever chooses on the game's behalf. An answer must name a row carrying the matching role chip; a name that doesn't exist, or exists but isn't chipped for the question asked, is treated as no legal answer — warned once (naming which rows ARE legal), falling back to the built-in reaction, never played anyway. Laubrary still decides WHETHER a hurt or death happens (Health reaching zero / taking damage, entirely untouched); the answerer only ever picks the APPEARANCE — proven by a probe where the character was killed regardless of which death row the answerer named. Per the owner's explicit T-0089 decision, there is deliberately NO built-in fallback answerer, not even for "there's only one option" — that has to be ordinary game code that explicitly answers. (4) **Default+override effects**, the plumbing finished — `FxEntry.overrides`/`FxOverride`/`FxEntry.Resolve` (the data model) landed in T-0092's commit but was never wired into the live play path; `ReactionFxPlayer.Fire` now calls `entry.Resolve(overrideName)` instead of reading `entry.fx` directly, for Immediate entries, OnFrame entries, and the end-of-reaction flush alike. `ReactionRequest` gains an `OverrideName` field (null/empty = no override, the path every pre-existing call site takes), carried through `Raise`/`TryRaise`. An override SWAPS which effect plays; it never gates one — an unmatched or absent name always yields the required default. Zoe window: every effect card gets a small "Overrides" sub-list (name + a `BuildManagedRef` effect picker, same pluggable-type UI every other field uses) below its own fields. Also, while re-verifying task 5 of the build plan (the death-linger timer starting at clip-end rather than moment-of-death): confirmed already fixed by T-0094's follow-up work, no new code needed. Verified via nine live spawn-based probes (targetPart refusal/acceptance at both the `ReactionFxPlayer` and `CompositeAnimatedView` levels, no-answerer/valid-answer/wrong-role-answer for both hurt and death, and override resolution both as pure data and end-to-end through `Raise`) plus a screenshot walk of the real Zoe window with a composite character and a role-chipped custom event.
- **Every Zoe now generates a compile-time list of the states it declares (T-0092).** Saving a Zoe writes a `<Zoe>.states.cs` sibling next to the asset — `ZoeStates.<Name>States.Fire`, plus an `All` array — so game code asks for a state by a constant instead of by a string literal. Rename a state in the Zoe window and the constant follows it, turning a rename from a silent runtime miss into a compile error. A sibling file rather than one central file on purpose: it joins whatever asmdef the asset already lives beside, which is the assembly the code using that character is in. Regenerated on save and re-verified after every domain reload (a hand-edited or merge-mangled file logs an error and is rewritten); an orphaned file is deleted when its Zoe is. Two ids that sanitise to the same identifier, or differ only in case, log an error naming both.
- **`ReactionRequest` — the in-params of a request to show a state (T-0092).** A `readonly struct` carrying `Position` (nullable, so "at the world origin" and "wherever the character is" are different answers — the old zero sentinel could not tell them apart), `Direction`, `Amount` and `Source`, with `ReactionRequest.From(in DamageInfo)` for the hit/death path. Deliberately not a widening of `DamageInfo`: that struct lives in Combat2D, means one damage event, and is passed around by every consumer project — a fire pose is not a blow.
- **`IFireStateSource` — game code chooses what firing looks like (T-0093).** A component on the character (or any parent of the weapon) implementing `string FireStateFor(in FireShot shot)` is asked for the name of the state to show on every shot, receiving the weapon, its `WeaponDef`, the live muzzle origin, the shot's aim and whether it was hitscan. With no such component the weapon asks for the reserved `"Fire"` exactly as before, quietly, so every existing project is untouched. With one, its answer is the name and there is **no fallback**: an empty or null answer means show nothing at all, because a hidden fallback would play a state in answer to a decision to play none. A chosen name that the character does not declare warns and lists the alternatives. Discovered via `GetComponentInParent`, matching `ICueSink`/`IVectorAimSource`/`IActiveWeaponSource`. Coverage does not depend on the game's perspective: the hook is on `ProjectileWeapon.Fired`/`HitscanFired`, downstream of `Combatant.aimDirection`, so player input, `autoFire`, a Daemon brain, Mirage's preview step and Target Practice are all covered by one path.
- **Zoe window: a reaction's `Stun` is finally visible and editable**, beside Clip / Lasts / Loops·Seconds on the same wrapping row. The field has existed on every reaction since reactions did and was reachable only through Unity's default inspector — which stopped being acceptable the moment it started being obeyed.
- **`ProjectileWeapon.ResolvedAimDirection()` is public.** It is the SHOT's aim (a sibling `IVectorAimSource`'s drawn barrel angle, then the owner's `aimDirection`, then the weapon's own field) — the right answer for a fire pose, where reading a spawned projectile's own direction would give one pellet's spread-jittered heading instead. Purely additive.

### Added
- **Pluggable `Zoe.playerController` (`IPlayerControllerSpec`), and move/aim/fire split into three independent driver components (T-0087).** A Zoe can now optionally declare a player controller the same way it already declares an AI brain (`[SerializeReference]`, same pattern as `IBrainSpec`) — `ZoeSpawner.SpawnCharacter` attaches it automatically for every caller, Mirage's Preview-in-Mirage included, so a character configured this way is driven by real input instead of Mirage's mouse-slider hand-control panel with zero per-tool wiring. The optional `Zoetrope.ZoeCharacter` bridge module supplies `TopDownPlayerControllerSpec`, which attaches `NewInputSystemSource` + `TopDownMotionDriver` + `AimDriver` + `ZoeWeaponDriver`. Move, aim and fire are three separate, independently swappable components (mirroring the existing `IZoeInputSource` split for raw input reading) rather than one bundle — `TopDownMotionDriver` used to also resolve aim direction, but aim reads identically in every 2D perspective (twin-stick/cursor aim doesn't care whether the mover below it has gravity), so it's now its own `AimDriver`, matching how `ZoeWeaponDriver` (fire) was already split out. A future perspective supplies its own `IPlayerControllerSpec` + motion driver while reusing `AimDriver`/`ZoeWeaponDriver` verbatim; a future aim style is a new component playing `AimDriver`'s role, with nothing downstream (fire, animation) needing to change.
- **Zoe editor: the "Player Controller" field is now actually visible (T-0087 follow-up).** `Zoe.playerController` (added earlier in T-0087) was configurable only via `SerializedObject`/reflection — `ZoeWindow`'s AI section drew `brain` but never `playerController`, so there was no way to see or set it from the Zoe editor itself. The AI section (now "AI / Control") draws both, side by side, via the same `BuildManagedRef` picker `brain` already uses.
- **Muzzle-on-fire VFX is now reliable regardless of whether a "Muzzle" MetaLayer is painted, and ProtoGuy's "Fire" reaction is authored (T-0087 follow-up).** Two of the same class of bug, both fixed the same way `MuzzleTracker`/`MuzzleVectorTracker` already fixed it for the projectile spawn point: timing and position were conflated, so an unpainted layer meant an effect silently never played at all instead of playing somewhere approximate. (1) `WeaponMuzzleCue`'s default path (no explicit `muzzleEventName`) now fires a weapon's muzzle VFX unconditionally on every successful shot (`ProjectileWeapon.Fired`/`HitscanFired`), at wherever its `muzzle` Transform currently is — already kept live-tracked-or-fallback by `MuzzleTracker`/`MuzzleVectorTracker` — instead of only doing so when the shooter has no `ICueSink`; a composite Zoe (has an `ICueSink`) previously got NO fallback at all and its flash simply never played until the layer was painted. The old frame-precise `ICueSink`/`OnMetaLayerReached` path is kept as an explicit opt-in via a non-empty `muzzleEventName`. (2) `EventContext.TryResolvePosition`'s `MetaPoint` placement now asks `IAnimatedView.GetMetaLayerKind` and samples via `TryGetMetaPointNearest`/`TryGetMetaVectorNearest` (same kind-aware, nearest-frame convention `ZoeMetaPositionResolver` already uses) instead of only `TryGetMetaPoint` on the current frame — which silently never matched a Vector-mode layer at all — falling back to the sprite's own visual centre when nothing is authored anywhere, so an `FxEntry` placed at MetaPoint always spawns instead of silently never firing. ProtoGuy's Zoe asset now authors the "Fire" custom event this made possible: a `bodyFx` Fake Light (`positionSource: External`, `metaLayerId: "Muzzle"`, an animated `add` pulse) plus an `fx` entry spawning the same Pyre blast his gun's own muzzle already uses (`placement: MetaPoint`, `metaLayerId: "Muzzle"`) — both configured entirely through the existing Zoe editor Custom-events UI, no code specific to ProtoGuy. Live-verified in Play mode: `weapon.TryFire()` now visibly lights ProtoGuy's sprite and spawns a real Pyre blast (screenshotted, not just checked by code), even though "Muzzle" still has no painted data — both effects currently land at his sprite centre as the documented fallback, and will automatically snap to the real muzzle point with zero further changes once someone paints that MetaLayer in the Laumination Builder.
- **SpriteFx `RelightModifier` can now take its Point-light position from a live external source instead of a painted curve, and Zoetrope wires "Muzzle" MetaLayers into it via a new "Fire" reaction (T-0087).** New opt-in seam `IExternalPosition2D`/`IExternalPositionResolver` (`Runtime/SpriteFx/IExternalPosition2D.cs`, same registration-seam shape as `ISpriteFxPreviewOverlay`): a modifier declares it wants a live 2D position under a caller-defined key; `SpriteFxFilter` (new `externalPositionResolver` field) feeds it fresh every tick, immediately before `Prepare()`/`Apply()` consume it — never cached across ticks, which matters because a `SpriteFxFilter.stack` is often a SHARED asset played by several characters at once, so a value that persisted between ticks could leak one character's position onto another's flash. `RelightModifier` implements it (`positionSource`: Painted/External, `metaLayerId`), falling back to its existing painted `lightX`/`lightY` on any tick nothing is fed. SpriteFx itself stays Zoetrope-agnostic — it never learns what a MetaLayer is. `Laubrary.Zoetrope.ZoeMetaPositionResolver` is Zoetrope's own resolver, converting a MetaLayer's WORLD-space point/vector (Point or Vector mode, whichever is actually painted, via `IAnimatedView.GetMetaLayerKind` — same "ask the data" convention `MuzzleTracker`/`MuzzleVectorTracker` use) into the target `SpriteRenderer`'s local half-frame-space via its current sprite bounds; `ReactionFxPlayer.PlayBodyFx` supplies it automatically to every `SpriteFxFilter` it plays a reaction's `bodyFx` through. New reserved custom-event id `WeaponMuzzleCue.FireEventId` ("Fire") — every successful shot now also calls `ReactionFxPlayer.Raise("Fire")` (harmless no-op if the shooter declared no such `NamedReaction`), so a character can author a "Fire" custom event (Zoe editor → Reactions → Custom events, already-existing UI) with a `bodyFx` stack containing a Fake Light set to Meta Layer / "Muzzle" and have it flash from wherever the muzzle actually is, entirely through existing authoring surfaces — no new field on `Zoe`, `WeaponDef` or `ZoeWeaponSlot`. Verified live: the position-source override, the world→half-frame conversion math, and the filter→modifier feed path all checked out under a synthetic probe; firing ProtoGuy's real weapon in Play mode confirmed `Raise("Fire")` fires, the `SpriteFxFilter` is created and correctly wired with the resolver — but ProtoGuy's own "Muzzle" MetaLayer turned out to have NO painted vector data (declared Vector-mode, `TryGetMetaVectorNearest` returns false even after real playback), so his existing Pyre muzzle flash has been running off `WeaponDef.muzzleOffset`'s fixed fallback all along, not real tracked data — a pre-existing content gap this pass surfaced but did not fix (painting it is Laumination Builder authoring work, not code).
- **`ZuiSectionToggleBar` adopted on the Zoe editor too (T-0084 follow-up).** `ZoeWindow` builds Identity / Stats / Look / Rig (only on a composite view) / Reactions / AI / Weapons / Cues through the same `Unit(host, label, build)` roster as the other five tools, with the bar itself sitting above the scroll view exactly as `ChunkWindow` places it. This meant unsealing `ZoetropeDefWindow<T>.BuildAsset` (it was `sealed override`, blocking `ZoeWindow` from adding the bar host above its own scroll view) — `WeaponDefWindow`/`AmmoDefWindow`, the base class's two other subclasses, keep the base's plain-scroll behaviour untouched.
- **`ZuiSectionToggleBar` adopted across Chunks, Launimator, Mirage, SpriteFx and Cartographer (T-0084).** PyrePlus/Pyre was the only tool with the full-width bar that shows/hides a whole `ZuiSection` at once as an alternative to folding each section's own header (T-0065); every other multi-section editor still made the user fold sections one at a time. All five now build the bar the same way Pyre does: a `Unit(host, label, build)` helper runs one section builder and, if it added a top-level `ZuiSection`, registers it under a label in a `_barUnits` roster, so the bar always reflects exactly the sections that exist for the current asset (a builder that adds nothing — e.g. Chunks' Fragment Slicer with no module assigned — registers nothing rather than parking a dead button). `ChunkWindow` bars all 18 of its sections (Tags, the eight composed-effect builders Slicer/Blasts/Movement/Formation/Layers/Splash/Timeline/Mirage, and the nine plain-debris dial sections), rebuilding the roster from scratch on every `BuildAsset`. `MirageWindow` bars View / Previewables / the conditionally-present Entry section. `SpriteFxStackWindow` bars Preview and Stack. `CartographerWindow`'s seven top-level panels (Level/Layers/Palette/Props/Decals/Tool/Tags) were framed `Z.BoxKeyed`s and had no fold-all story at all — converted to `Z.Section` (state keys preserved, so existing fold state carries over) so the bar has something to address. `LauminationBuilderWindow` is the one tool whose sections can be rebuilt by more than one path (initial `BuildUI` AND the in-place `Refresh()` that fires on selection/add-layer/mode-switch edits), so it re-registers `_barUnits` and rebuilds the bar host from both; disclosed trade-off there: the bar's active SOLO is an instance field, not persisted, so a structural edit that calls `Refresh()` drops any active solo (a section's own shown/hidden fold state and the Sections/Toggle-Bar mode choice are unaffected — both live outside the bar instance). All five reuse `ReserveBarHeight` (first written for Chunks, copied verbatim elsewhere) — pins the bar's host to the tallest height the bar has ever measured at the current width, so switching between Sections and Toggle-Bar mode (or soloing a section) can never change the geometry of the workspace below it.
- **ZUI `ZuiTimeline` / `Z.Timeline` + SpriteFx preview on ONE seconds clock (T-0074).** The SpriteFx Stack window presented its timebase as unrelated dials in two different sections — `Duration (s)` in a "Timeline" section, the between-loops rests beside the preview — with no picture tying them together, and the user's verdict was "very confusing". New reusable control `ZuiTimeline` (`Zui/Toolkit/ZuiTimeline.cs`, reached via `Z.Timeline(seconds, tooltip, onChanged, height)`): a `Painter2D`-painted scrub bar over a LOOP measured in seconds, split into consecutive coloured `ZuiTimelineSegment(name, seconds, color, tooltip)` bands drawn TO SCALE, with a playhead (dark stroke + light core, so it survives both light and dark bands) and grab handle, bottom ticks, numeric time labels that drop rather than overlap, per-band tooltips, and click-anywhere / pointer-captured drag — the same gesture idiom as `ZuiRampControl`'s strip. Generic by construction (a segment is just name/seconds/colour/tooltip); a zero-length segment is legal and paints nothing, and `Total <= 0` is guarded rather than divided. Hosts push their own clock in with `SetSecondsWithoutNotify` so a play tick cannot re-enter the scrub callback. SpriteFx then rewires onto it: the 0→1 `Life` MicroSlider and the multi-field rest/idle state machine (`_previewHeld`/`_previewPause`/`_previewIdle`/`_previewIdleLeft`/`_playProgress`) are gone, replaced by one scrub time and one play time in seconds, with a single `ResolveAt(t, out progress, out bypass)` that BOTH scrubbing and playback go through — so the picture you scrub to and the picture Play shows at the same instant can no longer disagree. The loop is TWO bands: `[0, active]` the stack playing (inclusive of its right edge, so scrubbing to the end of Active shows the FINISHED effect rather than the first instant of what follows), and `(active, total]` the stack BYPASSED entirely (the plain sprite — what `SpriteFxFilter.Restore` leaves behind), so scrubbing into the gap band now correctly shows no effect at all. `t` is clamped rather than wrapped, so the far right of the bar means the end of the loop instead of the start; playback wraps its own clock. Layout: the bar goes full-width BELOW the preview stage (a ruler needs length; the stage is a fixed 200pt square), and `Duration` — both the dial and the read-only hosted-event fact — moves out of the old "Timeline" section into ONE row under the bar beside `Idle gap`, each behind a chip in its own band's colour. That left the old "Timeline" section holding nothing but a hashing seed — a titled box announcing one field — so the section is gone and the seed moves to the head of the **Stack** section, which is the list it actually governs. The two tooltips that describe the loop (the bar's and Play's) are now assembled from the lengths that are actually set, so dialling Idle gap to 0 rewrites the sentence instead of leaving it naming a band that is no longer on screen. Every stale tooltip rewritten — including the scrub's, and `SpriteFxFilter`'s inspector, both of which still promised a stack-wide Life-remap envelope that was removed some time ago.
- **SpriteFx — an effect can now register its OWN preview overlay; the window hardcodes none of them.** `SpriteFxStackWindow`'s transport row carried a permanent "Light radius" toggle plus the ring/dot drawing code for it, visible even on a stack holding no Fake Light at all, and every future effect wanting a preview visual would have added another. Replaced by a registration pattern: `ISpriteFxPreviewOverlay` (`Runtime/SpriteFx/SpriteFxPreviewOverlay.cs` — in the RUNTIME assembly, unguarded, because the modifiers live there and cannot reference editor code) declares a label, a tooltip, a `WantsPreviewOverlay` answer the instance gives from its own configuration, and a `DrawPreviewOverlay(SpriteFxOverlayCanvas)`. `SpriteFxOverlayCanvas` wraps the preview's own pixel buffer and carries the primitives so nobody re-derives them: `Plot` (clipped, alpha-blended, alpha `max(dst, src)` so a marker survives over transparent pixels), `Circle` (step count scaled to the radius, so a large ring is not a dashed polygon), `Dot`, `Line`. Host side, `SpriteFxPreviewOverlays` (`Editor/SpriteFx/`) collects the enabled instances that currently want one, disambiguates several of the same effect (`Light radius 2`, with a tooltip naming both the ordinal and the stack position), keys each toggle's on/off state (default off) on the effect INSTANCE in a `ConditionalWeakTable` rather than on its label and ordinal — an ordinal is a position among the overlays that currently qualify and is recomputed on every collect, so a flag stored under one silently changes owner: disable the middle of three lights and the third inherits the second's flag, losing its marker or gaining one nobody asked for, and reordering, deleting and flipping an earlier light to Directional all do the same — builds the toggle strip as a `Z.SegmentedMulti` whose per-segment tooltips are composed for the state each toggle is in and refreshed on every click, and draws the switched-on ones. `OverlayGroupKey` lets a type say what several of itself should mean: `null` (the default, and Fake Light's answer) gives one toggle per instance, a shared constant collapses every instance behind a single toggle that draws them all. `RelightModifier` is the first implementer; its editor-only `TryGetPointGizmo` is gone, folded into `DrawPreviewOverlay`. The strip is a permanently reserved row at the BOTTOM of the preview's control column, holding a minimum height but free to shrink horizontally (a fixed height that also refused to wrap or shrink would have become an unshrinkable several-hundred-pixel control inside a column allowed to shrink to nothing, forcing a horizontal scrollbar — reflowing far more than the row ever could): it stays present and empty when the stack offers nothing, so a toggle arriving or leaving can never reflow the transport under the user's cursor. Side effect worth knowing: the light overlay now also draws on an UNPADDED stack (one whose effects need no margin), where it previously could not — `TryGetPointGizmo` bailed when the picture rect was unknown, while `Apply` had always fallen back to the buffer size; the overlay now uses the same fallback, so it can no longer disagree with what was shaded.
- **Pyre + ZUI — ONE ramp control replaces the vertical stack of per-stop cards (T-0064).** A `PyreRamp` used to fall through `ZuiReflect`'s generic `List<>` branch and render as a box titled `Stops  (8)` wrapping a card per stop, each a header row plus a `Pos` slider and a colour — ten of them for a Jet ramp, which the user's verdict called "impossible to work with". New `IZuiRamp` (`Zui/Scripts/Runtime/IZuiRamp.cs`, ZuiRuntime, global namespace: `Count`, `Get/SetPos`, `Get/SetColor`, `Insert`, `RemoveAt`, `Eval`, `BlendModeNames`, `BlendMode`) — an interface, not a type reference, because the Zui toolkit asmdef cannot see `Laubrary.Pyre` and must not be made to.
