None of the three pickers ever silently drops a value it does not recognise — an unknown current value is offered back with an "(undeclared)" / "(not authored)" tag and its own badge, so a rename reads as a broken link instead of quietly snapping to the first option, which is the same failure the picker exists to prevent. A folded cue's header states what fires it and what it does. Known remaining free-text path: the default Unity Inspector on a Zoe asset (no `CustomPropertyDrawer` was added for `CueBinding` — the Zoe window is the authoring surface).
- **PyrePlus — `ShapeForm.Inferno`, a volumetric fireball explosion (ported from the "fireball lab" HTML prototype, then reworked into a real PyrePlus form).** A new whole-layer form (`Runtime/PyrePlus/PlusInferno.cs`) rendering one-to-many contained sub-blasts as a per-pixel density/heat/smoke field: metaball clump lobes, a noise-torn angular silhouette (seeded directional-sine pack + looping value noise, ported verbatim to preserve the prototype's known-good look), rolling churn/warp, an asymmetric ignition flash, contained embers, cooling into soot, and pseudo-normal 3D lighting. Unlike Fire/Fireball it is **closed-form** — no sim, no replay harness, no cache — so scrubbing any frame is exact by construction (the prototype's torn shock-front RING pass was deliberately NOT ported — dropped on request). **The PyrePlus-native part:** the animatable dials are **ZUIValue envelopes over the layer's life** (Blast size / Flash / Churn / Rotation / Lift / Fire / Balance / Smoke / Hollow / Afterglow / Billow / Jagged / Cohesion — drop it late and the cloud visibly blows apart — / Clump spread / Darkness / Body, field ids −33..−41 and −43..−49, evaluated through the standard Eval funnel; the deliberately-plain remainder are event-time shapes (Bang speed/Recoil/Pulse), per-blast structure (Mutation/drift/jitter), and render-wide dials (Lighting/Contrast/Margin/Linger)), and **`infernoProgress` (id −32) is a TIME-REMAP envelope** — the identity curve plays real time, any other shape holds at full bloom, slows the smoky tail, or freezes a pose (a Static value), which only closed-form purity makes exact. **Placement is SWARM-NATIVE** (the bespoke pattern list was built first, then removed on feedback as confusing duplication): swarm OFF = one centred blast; swarm ON = every particle's spawn position + spawn moment ignites one blast via `ComputeSpawns`, so the swarm's Count / Circle / Polygon / Path / Custom shapes / spawn-timing envelope author every arrangement natively (a circle path = a ring of blasts detonating around it; area placement = a carpet; a tight cluster staggered by spawn timing = waves). Multi-blast swarms share **Mutation**, **Heat stacking** (overlapping blasts ADD heat) and **Balance drift / Balance jitter** (each sub-blast gets its OWN fire↔smoke character — drift slides it across the sequence, jitter randomises per blast). **Heavy smoke that STAYS**: the Smoke ceiling reaches genuinely heavy soot (the prototype topped out translucent), **Linger** holds it — at 1 there is literally zero tail fade (anything short compounds through the alpha chain; the shared Alpha envelope's own trapezoid still fades the tail unless flattened, said in the tooltip) — and **Afterglow** (an envelope) adds late residual core heat AFTER the hot-life chain so Cooling can't kill it: the low heat samples the ramp's dark end, the red-orange smoulder inside the lingering smoke. **Edge fade** kills the visible circle boundary a rim-slamming blast used to show: the containment feather was a fixed ~2px band, heat stayed fully hot up to the cut, and the FLASH was clipped by a hard `> safeRadius` reject with zero falloff — all three now share one inward-growing feather governed by the dial (default 0.35; 0 = the old tight band; nothing ever crosses the Safe margin), with heat additionally cooled toward the rim so the boundary never reads hot-to-nothing. **Hollow / Hollow rim** carve a controllable radial cavity (lobes remapped onto the shell, rim heat concentrated on the inner boundary — a burning shell/torus; Hollow is an envelope, so the cloud can bloom open into a ring). **Punch** (opt-in drama): the bang overshoots its radius and settles, ignition spikes the heat white-hot, the flash blows out bigger — plus a faster Bang-speed ceiling. **Body**: dense material-rich pixels saturate toward OPAQUE (0 = the prototype's gauzy look). **Colour comes ONLY from the Shape Fill**, evaluated through the FILL (mode + Adjust apply; a Solid fill = a single-colour flame) at 1 − heat (LEFT end = white-hot core); the flash glow is tinted from the ramp's own hot end (a blue fill gives a blue-white flash); smoke grey comes from the Darkness dial alone and never tints burning pixels (the ramp colour takes over hard as heat rises). **Geometry + pixel modifiers work on Inferno**: geometry inverse-warps every field sample per pixel in canvas-pixel space (the Fuse-pass precedent; containment computed from the warped point; flash/embers deliberately unwarped — inverse maps can't forward-transport stamped points), pixel modifiers recolour/drop each lit pixel (hash stream id −42); post modifiers already applied via the forced scratch. Seed = `Hash(spec.seed, layerSalt)` like Fire; `size` hidden (Blast size is a fraction of the safe zone). Verified: compile-clean; PNG film-strips eyeballed for all seven patterns + Punch + the Progress freeze + Body + a blue fill + a Wobble warp + a Posterize pass; `ZuiAudit` 0 findings on the expanded window.
- **Cartographer — `LevelFlowField`: shared room-scale flow-field navigation for tile levels.** Promoted from OutBurner's scavenge room (`RoomFlowField`, now deleted there) and generalized: one 4-way BFS flood from a TARGET cell over a `LevelInstance`'s walkable cells, shared by N followers — "from any cell, which neighbour is one step closer to the target?" — deliberately basic navigation, not a pathfinding framework (no per-agent paths, costs or smoothing; followers ask `TryGetNextWaypoint` each frame and fall back to direct chase on false, physics sliding covers everything finer than a cell). Walkability is the `LevelProcgen.Reachable` resolve pattern — a cell is blocked whenever ANY solid layer resolves a tile — resolved ONCE at `Bind(LevelInstance)` and cached, with `RefreshWalkability()`/`Rebind()` for when the level changes underneath (a procgen pass). The target is a POSITION, not "the player": `SetTarget(Vector3 world)` / `SetTarget(Vector2Int cell)`, rebuilt immediately on a target cell change and on an interval knob otherwise (0.3s default). Plus `IsWalkable(cell)` and `HasPath(world)` (does the built field reach here — sealed-pocket detection). Intended consumers: any mover or Zoe brain — a future Daemon behavior reads it the same way. OutBurner's `ScavengeGame`/`SwarmMover` now consume it with identical behavior (the game re-aims the field at the player every frame).
- **Fov — the TrueEye absorb pass: range knob, visibility queries, own-occluder verdict, reveal-all debug, first-seen event** (items 1-4 + 6 of `LAUBRARY_FOV_ROADMAP.md`; actor ghosts deliberately left boarded — a snapshot-of-an-animated-rig lifecycle is its own component design). **`FovConeRevealer.ViewRadius`** forwards the asset's live range knob (settable every frame like ViewAngle; `Attach` gains an optional trailing `viewRadius`). **`FovQuery`** answers the two point questions with their costs documented: `IsRevealed(world)` = LIVE line of sight via `TestPointVisibility` (geometry rejects + at most one raycast per revealer), `IsExplored(world, threshold)` = MEMORY via `SampleFogTextureColorAtPoint` (async-readback array read; blocking 1px ReadPixels only before the first readback lands — the FovRoomWorld recipe turns the readback on for exactly this). **Own-occluder exclusion, investigated:** the asset's sight rays are plain LayerMask queries from the eye with NO per-collider hook and no start-outside-UnobscuredRadius grace, and we don't patch the asset — the supported mechanism is per-REVEALER, layer-granular mask surgery: `IgnoreObstacleLayer(int)`/`IgnoreObstacleLayerOf(Component)` strip the owner's blocker layer from this revealer only (mask is read fresh per raycast, effective immediately), with the project-wide "Queries Start In Colliders" alternative documented, not set. **`FovDebug.RevealAll`** forces fog off through the asset's own appearance path (`SetFowEffectStrength(0)` — nothing torn down, exploration keeps accumulating, queries unaffected), `FovHideRenderers` additionally shows fog-hidden actors while it is on, and `FovRoomWorld.ConfigureOrCreate` re-asserts the flag after every (re)build because the asset resets the strength global on init. **`FovReveal.CellFirstRevealed`** (`event Action<Vector2Int>`) fires on a revealable cell's first threshold crossing in every mode — the reveal pop is now itself just the first subscriber, so fog-aware systems (discovery pathfinding, MarkDiscovered patterns) see exactly the crossings the pop celebrates.
- **Fov — the fog-of-war suite: room fog, aim-following cone, actor hiding and reveal policy, layered over the Pixel-Perfect Fog Of War asset.** Promoted from OutBurner's scavenge room into `Runtime/Fov` (`Laubrary.Fov`) as an optional bridge assembly: its asmdef references the asset's `Pixel-PerfectFogOfWar` asmdef by GUID, so Laubrary core never depends on the asset (a consumer without the asset should not take the Fov folder — the assembly cannot compile without it). Four pieces. **`FovRoomWorld`** is the serializable room recipe embedded as Inspector knobs: configure-or-create the scene's `FogOfWarWorld` for a rect room (near-black fog colour, resolution in texels-per-unit clamped between floors/ceilings, memory-floor regrow, world-grid pixelation + `RoundRevealerPosition`, and the `AllowBleeding`/`SightExtraAmount`/`EdgeSoftenDistance` lit-blocker tuning), with the non-knobs pinned and documented — `AsyncReadbackFogDataToCpu` ON (FovReveal's polling would otherwise block on ReadPixels) and **`HidersUseFogTexture` OFF as a SAFETY INVARIANT: hiders must gate on the revealer's live line-of-sight geometry, so no memory floor or reveal stamp in the fog texture can ever reveal a hidden actor**. **`FovConeRevealer`** is the aim-following flashlight cone: `Attach(parent, viewAngle, unobscuredRadius, obstacleMask)` builds it inactive-first on a child so every value lands before registration, `SetAimDirection(Vector2)` (or an optional `follow` transform) steers it, and the asset's serialized-protected `ObstacleMask` is reached by cached reflection. **`FovActorHide`** wires spawned actors to hide outside lit fog: a deferred attacher waits until the rig actually shows something, samples at the visual centre (roots sit at the feet), and `FovHideRenderers` re-asserts the hide each LateUpdate because animation players re-enable their renderers every tick — a one-shot toggle loses that fight a frame later. **`FovReveal`** is the reveal policy (LiveOnly / PartialMemory / SnapFull, threshold polling, memory-RT re-stamping each LateUpdate against the regrow decay, first-reveal pop, per-cell `Crossed` flags plus `RevealedCount`/`Completion`), generalized so the GAME supplies the revealable set — `Bind(world, cells, cellToWorld, cellSize)`, with a bounds+walkability-predicate convenience overload — because the module knows nothing about Cartographer. The move also fixes the NRE that logged on every wave spawn from `FOW.FogOfWarHider.CalculateSamplePointData`: a play-mode `AddComponent` leaves serialized fields at plain CLR defaults and Unity runs `OnEnable` inside `AddComponent` itself, so the hider dereferenced a still-NULL `SamplePoints` array before the caller could assign it — the hider now rides an inactive child GameObject until its sample array is assigned, so its first `OnEnable` already sees valid data and registers exactly once. No demo scene yet: the live consumer is OutBurner's scavenge room; a `Build Demo Scene` builder per authoring rule 8 is still owed.
- **Chunks — a live PREVIEW SUBJECT in the Chunk editor: see the slicing on a real sprite while configuring.** The Chunk window (which had no preview at all) gains a Preview section right under the Sampled Pseudo-3D Debris settings: a Subject sprite slot, a point-filtered stage showing the subject with the preview cuts OUTLINED on it, and a fixed strip of the actual debris those cuts produce — cut by the very runtime sampler (`SampledChunkSprites.Sample`, tint/edge modes and the baked modifier stack included), via a new `out RectInt cutRect` overload so the outlines and the cuts share one code path and cannot drift. Cuts are deterministic per preview seed (a Resample button rerolls), so tuning a tint or size dial re-cuts the SAME pieces and every `Dial` refreshes the preview live. The subject is EDITOR-ONLY state, never serialized into the ChunkSpec — it lives in EditorPrefs keyed by the spec's GUID via the new shared `PreviewSpritePrefs` (AssetKit.Editor), extracted from SpriteFxStackWindow's identical preview-sprite persistence (same key prefix there, so remembered sprites survive). Empty subject = the spec's own Sample Source stands in.
- **SpriteFx — stacks get an OWN CLOCK: `targetFps` on the stack asset (mirrored inline on the filter).** 0 (the default, preserving current behaviour) keeps the continuous evaluation — the filter re-evaluates every rendered frame. A rate steps the stack's time on a fixed 1/fps grid inside `SpriteFxFilter.Tick`, independent of whatever animation the filter rides — the design's use case: a fast brightness flicker over a slow 4-fps reel, or a deliberately chunky retro fade. Hashing effects (dither/dissolve) re-roll per STEP instead of per rendered frame, the step index is global across loop passes so a looped flicker doesn't repeat in lockstep, and an unchanged step over an unchanged source frame skips the whole pixel pass (the own clock genuinely ticks slower — it also costs less). The SpriteFx Stack window's Timeline gains the Step rate dial and its preview quantises identically (scrub and Play both WYSIWYG); the SpriteFxFilter inspector's inline Timeline gains the same dial. Note the design sketch said the default "inherits the driving laumination's fps" — the source read showed stacks never ticked at the reel's fps (evaluation was continuous per rendered frame, and SpriteFx deliberately knows nothing about reels), so the preserved default is continuous, not inherited.
- **Zoetrope — Body-SpriteFx event attachments gain PLAYBACK BINDINGS: Once / Loop / Run At End / Ping Pong, on the ATTACHMENT, not the stack.** Same stack, different bindings per event: `BodySpriteFxEffect.playback` — Once (a single play-through; enum value 0, so every pre-existing entry keeps its exact old behaviour) · Loop (repeat whole passes for the event's remaining duration, ending WITH the event) · Run At End (start late so the play ends exactly as the event does — a fade-out; the window is the new `fxSeconds`, 0 = the stack's own duration, an event shorter than the window compresses the play to fit) · Ping Pong (once forward at the start, once backward timed to the end). NEW cards from the Add-effect menu start on Loop (the design default), set at the creation site so old serialized data is untouched. Driven at runtime by new policy-free `SpriteFxFilter` primitives (`PlayLooping`, `SchedulePlay` with an optional reversed pass — which also fixed a latent retrigger bug where a restart could capture the filter's own filtered output as its source and compound); the event's duration reaches the effect via `EventContext.EventSecondsRemaining`, stamped by `ReactionFxPlayer` per fire (full length for Immediate entries, the remainder for OnFrame ones) from the new `IAnimatedView.GetClipSeconds` → `ZonedAnimationPlayer.GetClipSeconds` (frames ÷ fps·speedScale for a plain clip; 0 = unknown for a zoned strip, no clip or no view — every timed mode then degrades to a single play, as its tooltip states). The Zoe effect card draws the mode as a radio with a tooltip composed for the current choice, and the Fx Seconds field keeps its reserved space (visibility-hidden outside Run At End) so switching modes never reflows the card. The top-level `ReactionFx.bodyFx` slot deliberately keeps its plain once-play (it is a bare stack reference, not an attachment card).
- **Cartographer — the PROCGEN pass: props that scramble the level from a seed, with the mutation logic owned by the game.** `Prop` gains a `procgen` flag — a procgen prop is inert in a normal build and acts only when the pass runs. `PropBehaviour` gains `OnProcgen(level, placement, rng, seed)`: called by the new `LevelProcgen.Run(level, seed)` on the PREFAB's components directly (no scene object — implementations mutate the LEVEL DATA via `Paint(..., Origin.Generated)`/`ErasePaint` and never touch a transform), with ONE `System.Random(seed)` shared across the run so results are deterministic per seed; the raw `seed` rides along for behaviours that must agree across placements without a manager (derive a private Random from it — the shared stream's state depends on pass order). Run clears generated content first, so a re-run replaces the previous run, and returns how many procgen placements reached a behaviour. Also `LevelProcgen.CloneForRun` (a DontSave "(run)" copy — runtime rooms scramble the clone, never the shared asset) and `LevelProcgen.Reachable` (bounds-clamped 4-way flood fill treating any resolved cell of a named layer as a wall — the sanity assert for a pass that closes doors). The window's Generate box gains a **Procgen row**: Run procgen drives `LevelProcgen.Run` with the box's existing seed through the same undoable `Dial` + refresh path Generate uses. First consumers live in OutBurner: an aisle-gating maze pass and a one-of-N exit lottery that stores its verdict in each placement's existing `prefabChoice`.
- **Cartographer Tileset Builder — live grid playback, Tags mode with CRUD, and LevelTag→TileTag.** Two new toolbar toggles: **Show animated** plays every animated tile's frames in the grid and **Cycle randoms** cycles every Random tile's variants — the tileset previews alive instead of frame-one-of-everything (frame-guarded writes, hover preview keeps precedence). **LevelTag is renamed TileTag** (git-mv, GUIDs intact). Both tabs gain a **Tags mode** beside Select/Collision: pick a TileTag, click tiles (or clump cells) to toggle it, and carriers tint in the tag's own editorColor. A **Tags… CRUD card** manages the assets: rename-in-place (commits on Enter — each rename is an asset op), delete with confirmation, + New creates in Assets/Cartographer/Tags and activates. UI-guide compliance pass ran with it: two stale “slice a sprite sheet” tooltips fixed, ZuiAudit clean (0 issues; 5 persistently-folded elements skipped).
- **Cartographer — clumps paint into levels, route their own layers, and guess their own collision.** The palette gains a **Clumps strip**: click one and the whole locked arrangement becomes the brush. Every brush entry now carries a **layer shift** — 0 paints the active layer, +1 the next layer in front — so a wall clump stamps its body into the solid layer and its cap into the overhang layer in ONE gesture (relative, genre-neutral: the level's own back-to-front stack decides what '+1' means). Plucked tiles get a **collision default read from their art**: ≥90% coverage → Grid, ≤25% → None (cap slivers), between → Sprite outline. The Builder's Clumps tab gains **edit modes with assessment tints**: Layers mode (blue = stamped layer, orange = one in front; click toggles) and Collision mode (red = full square, yellow = sprite outline, green = pass-through; click cycles) — a clump's whole routing readable at a glance. Also the big rename: prefab Clumps are now **Props**, tile groups are now **Clumps** (see repo glossary).
- **Cartographer — PROPS: object-shaped content gets its own tab.** A `TileProp` is a LOCKED tile arrangement placed as one thing — a transparent single tile that layers on top (an urn), or a multi-tile object that only ever appears in its authored pattern (a 3×1 door, a 1×3 wall column). Props live on the Tileset (`props` + `propColumns`), keeping mixed footprints out of the loose-tile palette. The Tileset Builder's grid box gains a **Tiles | Props** tab: drag a sheet selection into the Props tab and it is plucked as ONE prop (member tiles become assets but never join the palette); hover/selection/movement are whole-prop by definition; right-click deletes (with its member tiles and atlas cells, confirmed first). The props grid shares the checkerboard, Lines/Alpha dials, seamless view, osc frames, zoom and middle-drag scrolling. ⏳ Next: painting props from the Cartographer palette; prop CONTENT editing is deliberately a later workflow.
- **Cartographer Tileset Builder — sheet zoom and middle-drag navigation.** The sheet canvas gains **wheel zoom toward the pointer** (1× fit up to 16×, the status line's "shown at %" tracks it) and **middle-drag panning** — the canvas has no scrollbars, so pan IS its scrolling, clamped so the sheet edge never detaches from the pane. The tileset grid gets its own **horizontal scrollbar** when zoomed cells overflow the pane, and **middle-drag scrolls** both it and the pane's vertical scroller at once.
- **Cartographer Tileset Builder — slicing is dead; curation is now PLUCKING.** The source sheet is just a picture: the builder reads pixels straight from the file — which can live **anywhere on disk**, project member or not (Open sheet…, or drag a .png straight from Explorer onto the canvas) — and lays the selection grid over it with no import, no sub-sprites, no wait. Minting **copies the selected cells' pixels into a small per-tileset atlas PNG** (`<Tileset> Atlas <size>.png`, one shared texture so painted levels stay batchable; append-only so existing tile references can never break): only curated art ever becomes a project asset, so a 13,000-cell bought pack costs exactly the tiles plucked from it. Tiles record **provenance** (source-sheet content hash + path + cells), which drives the already-plucked tint across renames/moves and lets a tileset reopen its source sheet. Sheet settings are remembered **by content hash in EditorPrefs** (works for out-of-project files; sidecars still maintained for project sheets), and the Recent… menu now lists sheets wherever they live. The whole pipeline on a 2496×1344 pack sheet: ~3 seconds, versus minutes of slicing import.
- **Cartographer Tileset Builder — recent sheets, grid zoom, live multi-tile thumbnails, and a lighter editor.** A **Recent…** menu lists every sheet with a provenance sidecar — pick one and its slicing settings load with it (the Animation Builder's reload-a-known-sheet convenience, powered by the shared provenance). The grid editor gains a **Zoom** dial (20–72px display cells — view only, tile data untouched). **Hovering (or single-selecting) a variant/animated tile animates its cell** — an animation plays its frames, a Random group cycles its variants — so a multi-tile reads as one at a glance. Performance: the oscillating overlays previously stroked every cell individually 20×/sec, which made the whole editor feel busy; the lattice is now batched into single fill/stroke passes and the pulse ticks at 10 Hz. ⏳ Queued next for the multi-tile suite: the pinnable actions card promoted to a reusable ZUI control with icon buttons, a contents popup for a selected multi-tile, and an edit popup (delete/reorder/duplicate the sprites inside a tile).
- **Cartographer Tileset Builder — the minting flow becomes Add / Random / Animated, and the sheet talks to the grid directly.** The Make-tiles buttons are now **Add** (a plain tile per selected cell; a single selection keeps the plain name), **Random** (one variant tile, policy from Settings) and **Animated** (one animated tile, FPS from Settings) — always present, disabled with the reason when unavailable; Policy and FPS fold away in a collapsible **Settings** subsection. New **Animation preview** box plays the current sheet selection as frames at the Settings FPS — audition a run of cells as an animation before minting anything. **Marquee moved to the RIGHT button** (the Animation Builder's own convention), freeing the left button for the new gesture: **drag a sheet selection straight onto the tileset grid** — dropping mints the cells as tiles and places the arrangement at the drop cell, atomically under the same Overwrite/Alt-push rules (a blocked drop deletes the just-minted assets again). Sheet selections now **oscillate** (translucent white↔black on the grid's own clock). And the grid's Copy/Delete/Paste moved OFF the window chrome into a **right-click actions card** — pinnable, draggable by its title bar, closing on outside click when unpinned — leaving the toolbar to settings and a status count only.
- **ZUI — `Z.Split`: the standard tool-window shape, as a control.** Controls in a left pane, workspace/preview in the right, divider draggable and REMEMBERED per window (`stateKey` → EditorPrefs). Wraps UITK's `TwoPaneSplitView`; documented in `zui.md`. The Tileset Builder adopts it (controls | sheet canvas), and the Cartographer window adopts it with a new right pane: a **live rasterised preview of the whole level**, refreshed on every edit and undo — the Scene view stays the canvas, the pane is the at-a-glance read.
- **ZUI layout rules — a new MANDATORY rule: contextual UI must never move the workspace.** Born from a real Tileset Builder complaint (selecting a tile popped the selection icons, wrapped the toolbar, and the whole grid jittered). Codified in `ui-layout-rules.md`: contextual toolbars are one fixed-height non-wrapping row holding every possible control with `visibility` doing the showing (Hidden keeps its space; add/remove and `display` reflow); status/hint lines are permanently reserved single lines that truncate rather than wrap; variable-width text goes last in its row; appearing panels overlay or use pre-reserved slots. The Tileset Builder's toolbar and status line, and the Cartographer palette's pattern readout, are the reference retrofits.
- **AssetKit — `SheetProvenance`: sprite sheets remember where they came from.** A sidecar JSON beside the texture (`<sheet>.png.source.json`) records the ORIGIN (path or URL), original filename, import date, and — the load-bearing part — the file's **content hash**: identity follows the pixels, so a renamed, moved or re-downloaded source is still recognised as the same sheet. The Tileset Builder stores its slicing settings (size/spacing/origin) there too and **restores them automatically** whenever a known sheet is assigned — nothing is configured twice; its new **Import sheet…** button brings a file in from anywhere on disk, checking the hash FIRST so already-imported pixels are reused (settings and all) instead of duplicated, and new files land in a `Sheets/` folder beside the tileset with their origin recorded. Launimator's URL download now writes the same sidecar (its slicing settings already persisted via its own region sidecar). Sheets never downloaded/imported through a tool don't have provenance yet — they gain it the first time the Tileset Builder slices them.
- **Cartographer — Alt-drag PUSHES instead of blocking or overwriting.** Holding Alt while dropping (or pasting) a selection in the tileset grid shoves the occupants of the landing cells one cell aside — cascading down the line, growing the grid when pushing downward — so a pattern can land in a crowd without destroying anything. The push DIRECTION is chosen by where the pointer sits relative to the hovered cell's centre (release left-of-centre and occupants shove left, and so on), the drop ghost turns cyan on occupied cells while Alt is held ("will push", not "blocked"), and the whole operation is atomic: a chain that would exit the grid refuses the entire drop. Probe-verified: cascading shove, downward growth, atomic wall refusal.
- **Cartographer — the tileset IS a grid now, and both windows treat it that way.** `Tileset` gains `paletteRows` alongside `paletteColumns`, with null entries as first-class EMPTY cells — patterns can breathe, and the grid grows downward on demand. The Tileset Builder's strip is replaced by a real **grid editor**: tiles at 1px gaps exactly like the sheet canvas; click / Ctrl-click / marquee selection; **drag a selection to move it, Ctrl-drag to duplicate it**, with a live drop ghost that shows blocked cells in red; an **Overwrite toggle** decides whether drops and pastes may replace occupied cells (off = only empty cells accept, atomically — a placement fully lands or nothing changes); and a **contextual toolbar** that populates with what is possible — grid width/height always, Copy/Delete when something is selected, Paste (click-to-drop, right-click cancels) when the clipboard holds a pattern. Delete clears CELLS only — tile assets are never deleted here. The per-tile × buttons and the broken list-reorder are gone. The Cartographer palette renders the **identical grid** — same columns, rows, empty cells, 1px gaps — because positions are the pattern data the multi-select brush reads. The sheet canvas's oscillating grid is now optional with **speed and amount dials** (a smooth black↔white swing; off = steady grey). Grid ops probe-verified: padding, copy, delete, atomic paste, overwrite blocking both ways, downward growth.
- **Combat2D — `AimIndicator`: the twin-stick aim pointer, as a tool.** A marker triangle riding a circle around a character, showing aim DIRECTION only — it never travels from its owner. The game feeds it a `Direction`; radius, size, colour, sorting and the circle's centre are dials. It centres on the character's VISIBLE body (measured from the first sprite it finds, overridable), because a root transform usually sits at the feet and a pointer orbiting the feet reads as broken — which is exactly how this was born: it was OutBurner-native for one day, misbehaved, and moved here as the generic tool it should have been from the start.
- **Cartographer — palette PATTERNS and a region-sampling picker: the temporary clump.** The window's palette now lays tiles in a FIXED grid (`Tileset.paletteColumns` — new field — with tile ORDER drag-editable in the Tileset Builder's strip), so neighbouring tiles form meaningful arrangements: **Ctrl-click several palette cells and they become a pattern brush** whose offsets mirror their palette positions — painting stamps the whole arrangement, ghost included, through every tool (drag, line, rect). The **Pick tool now marquees**: drag a rect over the level and the sampled tiles become the brush with their exact in-room offsets — a temporary clump lifted straight off the map, no asset created. Single-click and Ctrl-click eyedrop still pick one tile.
- **Cartographer — the Tileset Builder: sheet curation, automated where automation earns it.** A `ZuiAssetWindow<Tileset>` opened from the Cartographer window's Palette box ("Tileset builder…"): assign a sprite sheet and it is grid-sliced automatically (import settings pinned pixel-art-correct, PPU = tile size, empty cells skipped); then marquee cells on the canvas and say what they MEAN — *Tile from selection* (one tile, selected cells as variants in selection order), *Animated tile* (cells as frames), or *Tile per cell*. Minted tiles land in a folder beside the tileset and join it immediately. Cells the tileset already uses are tinted green, so across a many-sheet pack you always see what is still uncurated. Slicing is idempotent and REFERENCE-SAFE two ways: a rect already occupying a cell keeps its name and spriteID across re-slices (whatever convention named it), and cells are read back by RECT rather than name — so sheets sliced by older tooling are picked up as-is and re-slicing never breaks existing tiles. The design follows Launimator's Animation Builder, whose history already settled the question: whole-sheet auto-grouping was removed as a dead end — the human aims a marquee, the machine does the mechanical half inside it.: a script that acts on a part of the level.** Put one on a clump's prefab, stamp the clump, and when the level builds in Play mode the spawned component is handed its context — the `LevelInstance` that built it and the exact `ClumpPlacement` it came from (clump, cell, rotation, mirror) — then gets its `OnPlaced()` entry point. "Spawn the player here", "emit enemies from here" are all this shape, and unlike a named spot it scales to many copies of the same clump: each instance knows its own placement, nothing is name-keyed, so spot-name collisions cannot happen. Cartographer owns only the hand-off; the behaviours themselves are game code and the tool never learns what they do — the same interpretation line the tag system draws. Settings live on the behaviour's prefab (flavours = prefab variants); per-placement overrides deliberately wait for a second real use case.

- **Cartographer rebuild, phase 8 — procgen composes onto the asset, and hybrid levels fall out of it.** `LevelGenerator.Generate` gains a `LevelAsset` overload: same wandering-ground + banded-scatter algorithm, but every clump goes through the same `Place` call the editor uses, stamped `Origin.Generated`, with its prefab alternative chosen from the run's seed and STORED on the placement. Its "clear" step is `ClearGenerated()` — so regeneration replaces only what a generator made, and hand-authored paints, stamps and decals survive, which is the hybrid-level contract §3.9 demanded the model carry from day one. The recipe's rooms only replace the level's when the recipe actually has some, and the asset's explicit bounds are never touched — where a level ends is an authored decision. The window's Level box gains the ported Generate section (recipe, seed, roll, generate — replacing generated content only). Probe-verified: generated flags exact, authored content intact after three regenerations, same seed → identical placement list, different seed → different level. — metadata becomes queryable and VISIBLE.** The query API from the design lands on both sides of the asset/instance split: `LevelAsset.TagsAt(cell, layer)` (the resolved tile's tags + the source clump's tags + the layer's own tags), `HasTag(cell, tag)` across all layers, and `CellsWith(tag)` — pure functions of the asset — with instance-side twins that read the already-resolved build so gameplay can ask per-frame without re-resolving the level (probe-verified to agree with the asset exactly, including the whole-bounds case of a tag on a default-filled layer). The window gains the **Tags** box: attach/remove tags on the active layer, plus a **Show in scene** overlay that tints every tagged cell with its tag's editor colour (multi-tagged cells draw inset outlines per extra tag) and lists the hovered cell's tag names at the cursor — invisible metadata is unverifiable metadata, so the overlay is the phase's point, not a nicety. Overlay data is cached and recomputed on edit, never per-repaint. — the Scene view becomes the canvas.** The window's Tool box arms scene-view editing: **Paint** (click or drag; a drag never spams the undo stack), **Erase** (a cell's paint first, else the most recent stamped clump covering it — removing the whole placement and rebuilding, because two overlapping stamps share cells and erasing by footprint would punch holes in the other), **Line** (Bresenham, committed on release so the span can be adjusted mid-drag), **Rect** fill, **Pick** (eyedrops the resolved tile back into the palette; Ctrl-click picks from any tool), **Stamp** (the selected clump with rotation/mirror settings, drag-repeat tiling stamps edge-to-edge by footprint-overlap rejection — the old stamper's hard-won rule, ported), and **Decal** (drops the chosen sprite at the exact fractional click point on the active layer). A ghost outlines exactly the cells a gesture would write — preview and commit share the same cell lists so they cannot drift. Alt always erases, a locked layer refuses edits, and every commit is one `Undo.RecordObject` on the ASSET. Supporting it: `LevelAsset.ResolveCell` (the single-cell twin of `ResolveLayer`, probe-verified to agree with it on every cell) and `LevelInstance.RefreshCell` (re-resolves one cell in place — painting drags update one tile instead of rebuilding the level; the variant dict it updates is the same object the tile render reads). Probe-verified: paint lands the exact variant sprite with zero rebuild, erase falls back to the default fill, line cells are contiguous and endpoint-exact, rect fills are complete. The pointer gestures themselves are a faithful port of the old stamper's proven interaction and still owed a hands-on pass. — the window, with THE LEVEL as its subject.** `Laubrary ▸ Cartographer` now opens `CartographerWindow`, a `ZuiAssetWindow<LevelAsset>`: a thumbnail browser of levels, and for the open level a **Level** box (name, biome, explicit bounds, collision mode, and a *Preview in scene* button that builds/rebuilds a `LevelInstance` so the Scene view shows the real level), a **Layers** box (drag-to-reorder cards; the ACTIVE layer expands accordion-style to its tileset, default-fill tile, sorting, opacity and collision cluster — active is also what painting will affect and what the palette shows), a **Palette** box (the active layer's tileset as clickable swatches, variant tiles badged with their count), a **Clumps** box (the biome's structures as stamps, with *Edit clump…* jumping to the clump editor), and a **Decals** box listing placed free sprites. Every edit routes through one undoable `Dial` that also rebuilds the scene instance, so the Scene view tracks the asset live, including through undo/redo. **The old window loses its menu item and becomes the Clump Editor**, opened from the Clumps box — the fix for the old tool's core defect, where the "level editor" asked which *clump* you wanted and kept the level as a field inside a box. Verified with the window open and laid out: `ZuiAudit` reports zero findings at working width (the base asset-toolbar needs ~530px before its Rename/Delete buttons fit — a pre-existing `ZuiAssetWindow` trait, not new), and an eyeball pass of a real level built from warehouse art confirmed thumbnails, pickers, the accordion, the variant badge and empty-states render correctly. — variant policies resolve at paint time from the asset's own history, and tiles + decals animate.** `LevelAsset.Paint()` is now the everyday painting entry: it resolves the tile's variant policy and stores the result per cell (`SetPaint` stays for hand-overriding one cell's variant). Every policy is a **pure function of the asset's data** — RoundRobin cycles by how many paints of that tile exist, NeverRepeatLast excludes the most recent paint record's variant, Random/Weighted roll from an explicit stable hash of the cell (not `Vector2Int.GetHashCode`, which is an engine implementation detail) — so there is no hidden authoring cursor to lose to a domain reload, identical edit histories give identical levels, and repainting a cell lands the same variant. Verified in-editor: RoundRobin cycles exactly, NeverRepeatLast never repeats consecutively, a same-cell repaint is stable, degenerate weight lists are honoured absolutely, and a painted cell's sprite survives both `RefreshAllTiles` and a full instance rebuild unchanged — the flicker the design document warns about cannot happen. Animation: an animated `LevelTile` hands Unity its frames through `GetTileAnimationData` (a static tile declines), and an animated decal gets a `DecalAnimator` whose frame is a pure function of time — verified frame-exact including the wrap. Play-mode animation still owed an eyeball pass alongside the window work. — `LevelInstance` builds a `LevelAsset` into a scene, in the editor and at runtime.** One `[ExecuteAlways]` component on a Grid object constructs everything the asset describes: a Tilemap per layer (sorting, opacity, visibility), the default-fill floor, placed clumps, hand paints, decals (bare SpriteRenderers inheriting their layer's sorting, or prefabs in Play mode), per-layer colliders (composite merge, one-way effectors only in side-scroll levels), published named spots, and the per-cell variant registrations that let a shared tile asset render the exact variant each paint stored. Everything built is marked derived and **never saved with the scene** — the asset is the single source of truth, so a stale built copy in the scene file cannot become a second one. Collision narrowing now composes both tag sources: on a solid layer with a `solidTag`, a cell collides if its TILE or its source CLUMP carries the tag, and a tile whose own shape is None never collides. `ClumpPlacement` additionally stores which prefab choice it resolved to at place time (same determinism rule as paint-time variants — a rebuild must never re-roll a level), and the collision mode (top-down vs side-scroll) moved onto the level asset where the legacy scene component used to keep it. Verified in-editor against a hand-constructed level: default fill, stored-variant rendering, clump cells landing on their layer, tag narrowing, collider presence only where solid, spot world positions, decal sorting, and rebuild idempotence all asserted.
- **Cartographer rebuild, phase 1 — the level becomes an ASSET and the data model gains tilesets, variants, animation, decals and per-element authorship.** Per `CARTOGRAPHER_DESIGN.md` (the 2026-07-31 first-principles rebrief): **`LevelAsset`** is now the authoring truth — a browsable, previewable, pickable asset holding layers, painted cells, clump placements, free-floating **`Decal`s** and the level's Rooms, with explicit `RectInt` bounds; a scene will consume it through a `LevelInstance` (phase 2) instead of owning the level as a MonoBehaviour. **`LevelTile`** is the new atom: one tile asset carrying interchangeable **variants** with a pick policy (Random / RoundRobin / NeverRepeatLast / Weighted — resolved at PAINT time and stored per cell, never re-rolled at draw time where chunk refreshes would flicker), frame **animation** via `GetTileAnimationData`, a collider shape, and gameplay tags. **`Tileset`** is the palette a layer paints from, and a biome now lists tilesets (its old flat tile list stays until the generator is ported). The new **`LevelLayer`** is pure asset data (tileset, default-fill tile, sorting, opacity, visibility, lock, collision flags) — the Tilemap that renders it belongs to the scene instance. Every paint, placement and decal records **`Origin` (Authored / Generated)**, so a hybrid level can regenerate by removing only generated content (`ClearGenerated`) — one byte per element now instead of a rewrite later. `ClumpTag` is promoted to **`LevelTag`** (same asset GUIDs, tags now attach to tiles, decals and layers too), `Clump` itself survives unchanged as the multi-tile structure, and the old scene-bound `CartographerLevel` keeps compiling (its inline layer type renamed `LegacyLevelLayer`) until the migration phase retires it. Level thumbnails rasterise the level itself through the shared preview path — a too-big level renders a centred 1:1 crop rather than a smear — and `CartographerPreview` now reads non-CPU-readable (normally imported) sprites through `PreviewTex` instead of silently skipping them, so tile/clump/level thumbnails survive meeting real art. Verified in-editor: the full model round-trips through serialization (variant bytes, Origin flags, rooms, bounds all present in the saved YAML), layer resolution honours default-fill → placements → paints order, and generated-only clearing leaves authored content alone.

### Changed
- **Combat2D — hit filters COMPOSE instead of competing, and a weapon can be given a per-shot motion factory.** Two small changes, both driven by building a real depth shooter on top of Combat2D. **(1) `Combatant.HitFilters`** replaces a single `GetComponent<IHitFilter>()` with `GetComponents`, and `Combat.TryDamage` now requires **every** filter to agree — a filter is a *veto*, so more than one can apply to the same hit. This was a latent bug, not just a limitation: whichever `IHitFilter` component happened to be found first silently won and any other never ran at all. It matters now because the filters answer different questions and both are true at once — `ReelHitFilter` asks "did the sprites really touch", while a depth-band filter asks "are these two even at the same distance", which a depth shooter needs because **Combat2D collides on XY only** and two things at different depths share a collider position. `Combatant.HitFilter` (singular) is kept, returning the first, so existing callers are unaffected. **(2) `ProjectileWeapon.motionFactory`** — an optional `Func<IProjectileMotion>` consulted per shot, overriding whatever the ammo template carries. Deliberately a **factory, not an instance**, because `IProjectileMotion` holds per-shot state (`Init` stores origin/direction/target) and one shared instance would have every projectile in flight fighting over the same fields. It lets a *scene* decide how shots travel — a depth shooter wanting `DepthMotion` — without duplicating the ammo asset per level type, which is what keeps a looted weapon carryable between scene types. Re-read every shot rather than baked into the pooled template, so flipping a mode mid-play takes effect immediately instead of only for projectiles pooled afterwards.
- **Cartographer — `RoomDirector` is renamed `Arena`.** An **Arena is where a scene's play happens**, whatever its size, shape or genre — a single walled room, a scrolling stretch of street, or a peaceful platforming stroll are all Arenas; the old name described the mechanism (it directs Rooms) rather than the thing. **`Room` survives as the sub-unit**: an Arena *contains* Rooms, each carrying its own exit condition, scroll behaviour and camera mode. This fixes the division of labour the tool trio rests on — **Cartographer builds the space, the Arena decides how play moves through it, and the game supplies what play actually is.** `RoomCameraBinder` deliberately keeps its name (what it reads is the *Room's* camera mode; the Arena is only where it looks that up) but its field is now `arena`, not `director`. **Done while the rename was free:** no scene, prefab or asset in this repo or in OutBurner referenced the type, so no `[MovedFrom]` shim was needed — and the window was about to close, since OutBurner's Scavenge scene is next to adopt it. The file kept its GUID via `git mv`, so any reference that did exist would have survived regardless. No behaviour change: the class body is byte-identical apart from its declaration.

### Fixed
- **PyrePlus `Playback3D` — the 3D preview now looks like the VFX pack it is previewing, and the pixelated preview is no longer squashed.** Reported as "the 3D looked nothing like what I would expect that VFX pack to look like, and the pixelated version looked like it was squashed together into one line". Six separate causes, all verified against the *Vefects Fire VFX (URP)* pack and its own demo scene (which the new reference rig `Assets/Dev/PyrePlusPlayback3D/Playback3DVfxTest.unity` reproduces): **(1)** the preview rendered raw with no colour grade, but these packs author their fire materials at emissive intensities of 33–123 and ship a Volume with Bloom + ACES Tonemapping — a `PreviewRenderUtility` scene gets neither, and a global Volume cannot be added to one without leaking into every other editor camera, so the grade is now reproduced explicitly (HDR `ARGBHalf` render target + a new `PyrePlusPlayback3DPost.shader` doing threshold/downsample/upsample bloom, exposure, tint and a full ACES matrix fit; the cheap per-channel Narkowicz approximation was tried first and is wrong here — it leaves a core measured at RGB (24.9, 14.4, 0.0) as flat saturated yellow instead of desaturating it to white-hot). **(2)** `Tint` was assigned straight into every `ParticleSystem` main module's `startColor`, REPLACING the pack's authored per-particle colour gradients with one flat colour; it is now a multiply on the finished image, so white leaves the prefab untouched. **(3)** The preview cleared to `PreviewRenderUtility`'s default grey, on which additive fire loses its glow and black smoke reads as a dirty blob; it now clears to black with alpha 0, so the result also composites over the PyrePlus backdrop like every other shape. **(4)** `Simulate` was called on EVERY `ParticleSystem` including nested children, re-running each child a second time from t=0 — the Vefects hero prefabs have 5–11 systems each; only the top-most systems are driven now (`withChildren: true` already covers the rest). **(5)** The camera was at a hand-tuned fixed position that clipped tall effects; it now auto-frames from real particle positions (`ParticleSystemRenderer.bounds` comes back wildly oversized on these packs) using a **percentile** of the particle spread rather than the maximum, so the dense body of the effect fills the frame instead of being shrunk to a dot to accommodate the smoke column and stray sparks, and it satisfies both axes so a narrow preview island no longer crops the sides. A new **Zoom** dial scales that automatic fit. **(6)** The pixelated grid was forced SQUARE regardless of the preview island's aspect and then drawn back with `ScaleToFit` — that is the "squashed into one line"; the grid now takes the requested size on the long edge and follows the island's aspect on the short one.
- **PyrePlus `Playback3D` — the pixelated preview now honestly represents what is under each pixel.** It downsampled in one bilinear `Blit` from ~936×576 straight to the grid, which reads only a 2×2 neighbourhood per output cell out of the ~19×19 it covers, so most sparks and flame licks were never sampled at all. It now halves progressively so each cell averages its whole block, draws at a whole-number scale (a fractional scale gave some cells 7 screen pixels and some 8, visibly wobbling the grid), and gains an optional **Colours** posterisation dial — without it a shrunken render is a small photo rather than pixel art. New `playbackZoom`/`playbackGlow`/`playbackPixelLevels` fields on `PyrePlusLayer`; the status label now prints the real grid dimensions instead of claiming a square one.
- **PyrePlus `Playback3D` — one bad prefab can no longer permanently break the Pyre Plus window.** The `BeginPreview`/render/`EndPreview` sequence had no `try/finally`, so a throwing render (a material on a mismatched render pipeline is the realistic trigger) left the camera pointed at the preview's own render target, `PreviewRenderUtility` stuck in its "begun" state and the wrong `RenderTexture` active — after which every later repaint drew into a stale target and the rest of the window silently stopped rendering. Also: the preview prefab is instantiated with `Object.Instantiate` instead of `PrefabUtility.InstantiatePrefab`, which was dropping the VFX into the user's ACTIVE SCENE (dirtying it) before moving it into the preview scene; `_BloomTex` is unbound before its pooled render textures are released, so the persistent material cannot end up holding a destroyed texture; pooled temporaries have their filter mode restored before release; and a missing post shader is cached instead of re-running `Shader.Find` on every repaint.
- **ZUI value control — the config menu (mode switch) is reachable again in Static mode.** In Static mode the whole row is one MicroSlider, and its own right-click display-options menu swallowed EVERY right-click — so once a value was switched to Static there was no way back to Envelope/Steps (confirmed live across Swarm/Fill rows). The value control now claims the right-click AHEAD of the child slider in Static mode only (a `TrickleDown` handler), so its mode/multiplier/copy-paste menu opens; the other modes keep child-first behaviour (envelope point menus still win on themselves).
- **ZUI envelope — a curve can now be deleted down to ONE point (a flat line = a constant), instead of being floored at two.** The `ZUIValue` curve editor passed the default `minPoints = 2`, so the last two points refused to delete — you couldn't reduce an envelope to a constant from inside the curve editor. It now opts into `minPoints = 1`: delete one of two points and a single point remains, which the runtime already evaluates as a flat/constant line (`ZUIEnvelopeEvaluator` returns `points[0].value` for `Count == 1`), so dragging the lone point up/down sets a horizontal line — the same output as a Static value, authored in place. Double-click empty space adds points back, so it's fully reversible. Only the `ZUIValue` curve editor opts in; other `ZuiEnvelope` callers keep their own floor. Verified in-editor: delete leaves 1 point (a second delete is refused), eval is constant at the remaining value, and re-dragging the dot moves the constant.
- **GIF export — soft edges no longer get sliced into a hard silhouette (ordered alpha dithering).** GIF transparency is **one bit**: a pixel is either fully opaque or fully invisible, so the exporter's flat `alpha >= 128` cutoff turned every feathered edge into a cutout. That was always true, but it only became glaring once Inferno's edges got genuinely soft — on a default soft-edged blast **26% of the canvas** sits in the partial-alpha band the format cannot represent, and all of it was being hard-thresholded. `PyrePlusGif` now dithers that band with a Bayer 4×4 ordered matrix (threshold 8..248, so alpha 0 is never kept and 255 always is), looked up in **source-pixel space** so the stipple stays at the art's resolution and an upscaled export keeps whole solid pixels. `BuildPalette` shares the same opacity test as the encode pass — otherwise the newly-surviving edge colours would be missing from the palette and encode as index 0. Exposed as a **GIF dither** toggle beside GIF scale (`previewGifDither`, default ON, export-only — the live preview is unaffected). Verified by simulating the quantisation both ways: the flat cutoff loses the entire smoke shell, the dither keeps it as a visible fade.
- **Inferno ignored the swarm's LIVE whole-cloud Scale and spin — the preview's spawn dots moved but the blasts didn't.** `RenderLayer`'s Inferno branch built its blast origins from `ComputeSpawns` raw, skipping the live `swarmScale` / `swarmTurn`+`Tilt`+`Roll` that **every other consumer applies**: `RenderSwarm` does it for all particle forms (`dp = centre + scale·spin(offset)`), `BuildFireEmitters` does it for swarm-Fire, and the preview overlay's `DrawSpawnDots` does it via the shared `ApplySwarmSpin`/`ApplySwarmScale` helpers. So the dots (correct) and the blasts (stale) diverged the moment the live Scale left 1 — raising it spread the dots while the explosion stayed put, and a spinning cloud left its blasts behind. Fixed by applying the same two helpers in the same order at origin build. Verified: at Scale 2 the dot spread doubles exactly (19→38px) and the rendered lit bbox follows (40→54px), with every dot landing on a fully opaque rendered pixel at both scales.
- **Inferno — a 20-point authoring pass: the one-frame SNAP fixed, the "stone disc" look fixed, and the fire/smoke dials rationalised.** **(1) The snap** (the cloud visibly jumping to a different shape in a single frame, mid-animation) was `churnTime` being wrapped with `% 1`: `WaveNoise` advances its phase by `TAU·t·speed` with a NON-INTEGER speed, so wrapping t from ~1 back to 0 jumped every wave by a non-multiple of TAU — a hard discontinuity that fired whenever `churn·local` passed 1. Time is now unwrapped (continuous); `LoopValueNoise`'s cos/sin orbit is periodic in t anyway, so nothing else changed. Pulse was accidentally masking it. **(2) The hard-edged "stone cake" silhouette**: `EdgeFade` conflated two different jobs and is split — **Edge softness** is now the BLAST's own rim falloff in absolute canvas terms (size-independent, so a small blast is not a hard pellet and a big one is not a disc), while **Frame fade** is purely the canvas-border guard that takes anything nearing the Safe margin to full transparency. **Smoke spread** gives the soot its own wider, softer boundary so heavy smoke FRAMES the fire and feathers into the background instead of stopping at the flame's silhouette, and **Core density** refills the middle (the cavity and fine-noise bites are strongest exactly where the cloud is thickest, which was hollowing the centre even at Hollow 0). **(3) The fire/smoke model** — **Balance is REMOVED**: it re-weighted the fire↔smoke split a second time from a different section, so Fire, Balance, Cooling, Smoke and Linger all appeared to control the same thing. Fire and Smoke are now independent amounts (each the ONE dial for its material), grouped into their own **Fire** and **Smoke** sub-boxes with Heat pockets/Cooling/Core glow under the first and Spread/Darkness/Linger/Body under the second; the per-blast Balance drift/jitter become **Character drift/jitter** (neutral = exactly the layer's dials). **Also:** Lift removed (an explosion has no up); Billow's authored range remapped so the old ceiling sits at ~0.67 with real headroom above; **Hollow rim** promoted to an envelope and joined by **Outer rim** (a burning surface rather than a lit disc); **Flash reach** and **Flash softness** separate the flash's size and alpha falloff from its brightness; **Die out** dissolves the whole effect to nothing over the final fraction of the timeline so it ends on its own instead of being cut off by the last frame; **Body** now blends toward solid and lifts the mid-range instead of a Max() that alpha usually already exceeded; Clumps/Clump spread strengthened (wider size range, lobes pushed 1.3× further, the angular term doubled) so they restructure the cloud rather than nudging its texture. New envelopes take field ids −50..−54.
- **Inferno — ~2× faster per frame (and the preview-lag question answered).** Two provably output-identical wins: the clump loop's position/radius math (5 trig + 2 smoothsteps per clump per pixel per blast — over half the form's transcendental cost) is **hoisted out of the pixel loop** into per-blast precomputation, and a **conservative pre-reject** now runs before the four-noise prologue (the churn warp displaces a sample by at most 0.5·warp per axis, so anything beyond the disc bound plus that diagonal is provably rejected later) — which skips the whole 10-trig/8-hash prologue for pixels belonging to a different blast, the dominant cost in a multi-blast swarm. Measured after: 128px single blast **13 ms/frame**, 128px 8-blast swarm **28 ms**, 64px 8-blast **5 ms**, 256px 8-blast **122 ms**. So preview playback is smooth up to ~128px and only stutters at large canvases — and it is a PREVIEW artefact either way: playback re-renders a full frame every tick, whereas the filmstrip and the GIF export play from pre-baked frames at zero render cost.
- **PyrePlus — the selected layer now persists on the asset (Pyre parity).** `layerSel` was transient window state, so reopening the window or revisiting an asset reset the Shape/Swarm/Matte/Modifiers sections to a different layer; it now lives on the spec as `previewLayerSel`, exactly like Pyre's `Pyre.previewLayerSel`. `[HideInInspector]`, cosmetic, never read by the renderer.
- **ZUI value control — Static mode was a ONE-WAY TRAPDOOR: the config menu was unreachable, so a value switched to Static could never go back to Envelope.** The "right-click replaces the ⋯ button" change registered the config menu in the BUBBLE phase so child controls keep their own right-clicks — but the Static face IS one MicroSlider spanning the whole row, and the MicroSlider's display-options menu stopped every right-click before it bubbled. Net effect: no ⋯ button + no reachable right-click = no mode menu; every currently-Static value across ALL windows (Swarm, Fill, everywhere) read as a plain static slider, which is what made "most sliders look static-only". Fixed with a TrickleDown-phase handler active ONLY in Static mode (the config menu is the essential one there; the other modes keep child-first right-click so envelope point menus still win). One conditional — no API change.
- **Modifier params — the MultiCont overhaul (audited by subagents, verified byte-identical on all 61 authored assets).** The complaint "most modifiers only have static values" turned out to be four real bugs plus 15 genuinely-static params, all fixed: **(1) destructive default ranges** — `ZuiReflect` rendered every reflected `ZUIValue` modifier param on the control's default 0–10 track (silently clamping `Rotate.degrees` ±180, `Turbulence.zoom` 24, `Kaleidoscope.arcDegrees` 360 … destructively on first drag); every `ZUIValue` param in `SpriteFxModifiers.cs`/`PyreSimulationModifiers.cs` now carries a real `[Range]` lifted from Pyre1's hand-tuned editor rows, which the reflection drawer honours. **(2) Steps rendered flat** — both renderers' `Eval` fell through `Mode.Steps` to the static value, so an authored step-sequencer pattern silently drew as one flat number; both now hold-step it across the frame-baked timeline exactly like Curve. **(3) Min-Max offered where it can't work** — a modifier param Evals frame-global with no frame in the hash, so Min-Max resolves to ONE constant for the whole animation; the PyrePlus modifier drawer now hides that mode (new `ZuiReflect.Options.ConfigureValue` hook; Pyre1's rows already passed `allowMinMax:false`) and the meaningless runtime Duration/Warmup/Loop row. **(4) stale "static value only" comments** — the PyrePlus modifier UI's file header still asserted the closed prototype limitation (almost certainly what seeded the perception); deleted, along with the dead `FloatWrapperProperty` wiring. **The 15 param conversions** (float → `ZUIValue` envelope via the proven MetaOrb frozen-legacy + companion + upgraded-flag recipe, so existing assets migrate losslessly on first touch): Outline inner/outer softness + both curves + alpha threshold, Ground surface + bury, Rotate pivot X/Y (now a `Z.Value2D` pad in Pyre1, animatable), Bloom threshold, Curl warp, Voronoi spread softness, AlphaMask offset X/Y + sharpness, Sunburst sharpness, PointBlast arc, PixelFluid child-shed — each with a fresh field id, Pyre1's hand-written rows updated to edit the companions. Also: `Sfx.CloneVal`/`Layer.CloneVal` no longer drop `smoothness`/`steps` on Duplicate (switched to the `CopyFrom` form), `KaleidoscopeModifier` gains its missing `[Serializable]`, `ZuiReflect` renders `List<Vector2>` elements (Smudge stroke points were empty cards) and strips the `…Value` companion suffix from reflected labels. Deliberately untouched: the discrete counts (rays/rings/arms/levels — animating them reads as flicker, documented), DropShadow (integer-quantised, zero uses), ColorRemap (no Prepare plumbing at all — its own future task). **Verified:** FNV-hash of every frame of all 29 Pyre + 32 PyrePlus authored assets identical before/after; compile-clean; the PyrePlus modifier cards eyeballed (correct ranges, clean labels, a Ground + Outline stack rendering live on an Inferno layer); `ZuiAudit` 0 findings.
- **Combat2D — a projectile no longer sails through a target whose pixel-perfect filter said no at the edge.** `Projectile` tested a hit exactly ONCE, on `OnTriggerEnter2D` — and the contact point at entry is the target collider's edge, which for a sprite with padding is transparent, so `SpriteAlphaHitFilter` vetoed and the bullet passed through the whole body untested ("bullets mostly go through the imps"). The veto is no longer final: `OnTriggerStay2D` keeps re-asking while overlapped, so the first frame the bullet's contact lands on an opaque pixel connects. Oversized bullets made it worse by putting the centre far from the body — the bullet *visual* size is ammo data and was fixed separately game-side.
- **Cartographer — the Tileset Builder's grid and selection are now IN FRONT of the sheet and unmissable.** The grid/selection overlays were painted via the stage's own `generateVisualContent`, which renders BENEATH children — and the sheet Image is a child, so everything drew invisibly behind the art (the exact trap the old ClumpStage documented; re-learned the hard way). They now live on an overlay child kept in front, the **grid oscillates black/white** so it reads against any art, selection carries a double (black-under-light) outline, and the builder gains **Spacing** (horizontal/vertical pixel gaps) and **Origin** (top-left margin) controls threaded through slicing, loading and the canvas — sheets with padding now slice correctly. The "In this tileset" strip is **drag-to-reorder**, because tile order is now the palette's pattern layout.
- **Cartographer — palette and clump swatches no longer go dark after Play or a domain reload.** The swatch thumbnails are runtime textures under a RETAINED visual tree: a play-mode transition destroyed the unflagged textures while the swatches kept pointing at them, so the grids rendered dark until a stray click happened to rebuild them. Fixed on both sides: thumbnail textures are `HideAndDontSave` (transitions no longer destroy them), and the window rebuilds its palette/clump grids on every enter/exit of Play anyway, so a texture created on the other side of the boundary can never linger dead. Probe-verified by destroying every cached thumbnail and firing the transition handler — all regenerate.
- **ZUI section-label — a foldable `Z.Text(.., ZuiText.Section)` heading placed INSIDE a `ZuiSection` no longer destroys the whole section when folded.** The Zoe editor's "Reactions" section (which holds "Hit"/"Death" section-label sub-headings) vanished irrecoverably when its "Hit" heading was folded — leaving no header to click to reopen it. Cause: `ZuiSectionLabel.Apply()` walked `parent.hierarchy` to hide its following siblings, but a label inside a ZuiSection has `parent` = the section (its LOGICAL owner via `contentContainer`) while the label physically lives in the section's `_body`. So `parent.hierarchy` = `[header, body]` didn't contain the label, `IndexOf` returned −1, and the hide-loop (from index 0) hid the section's OWN header + body. Fixed by iterating the label's PHYSICAL parent (`hierarchy.parent`, the element that actually holds it) so it hides its real content siblings — plus a guard (bail if the label isn't among its parent's children) and a defensive skip of any `zui-section__header`/`__body`. Verified: folding "Hit" now hides only the hit content (clip / effects / add-button) and the Reactions section stays intact and reopenable. Flat heading usage (Pyre / Choreographer / Launimator / Rulesets, where `parent == hierarchy.parent`) is unchanged.
- **Zoetrope Target Practice — a clip-less death now TRULY shows nothing (the earlier #6 hide was defeated by the animation player).** A death with no death clip is meant to blank the body so it can be replaced by an explosion FX — but the Floating Disc (a Zoned Launimator view) still flashed back to its idle first frame on death. `TargetPracticeController` only disabled the body's `SpriteRenderer`s, and the STILL-RUNNING idle animation's `ZonedAnimationPlayer.PushSprite` re-asserts `renderer.enabled` every frame, so the hide was undone on the very next tick. Fixed by adding a real "show nothing" capability rather than a one-shot toggle: `IAnimatedView.Hide()` (stop playback + blank), implemented by `AnimatedViewRelay` → new `ZonedAnimationPlayer.Hide()` (Stop + disable the base/layer renderers — the **Stop** is what makes it stick, since a stopped player's `Tick` early-returns and no longer re-asserts visibility). `TargetPracticeController` now calls `view.Hide()` before `SetBodyVisible(false)` on a clip-less death (the latter still covers a plain `SpriteView` with no `IAnimatedView`); a later Idle/respawn `PlayClip` re-shows it. Verified against the real Floating Disc reel: disabling the renderer mid-play gets re-enabled by the next tick (the bug), whereas `Hide()` stays blank across five ticks and respawn re-shows.
- **ZUI — a tool's editor arrangement now persists WITH THE PROJECT (`ZuiToolStateStore`).** Window arrangement lived in `[SerializeField]` fields and `EditorPrefs`, so it was per-machine and invisible to everyone else — set a preview backdrop, and a teammate (or you, next clone) saw a different tool. There is now one committed store asset in the host project (`Assets/Laubrary/ZuiToolState.asset`, created on demand — no menu item) holding each tool's live arrangement, keyed by the tool's own stable slug so a window retitle can't orphan it. Values are generic (flags / numbers / vectors / colours / object refs) so the toolkit never has to reference a consumer to store its state. Sibling to `ZuiViewStore`, and the split is deliberate: that one holds NAMED presets a user picks, this one holds the single arrangement nobody names. TextSplash's preview backdrop is the first adopter. **Global per tool, not per asset** — the arrangement worth restoring is "how I like this window", and per-asset state would need a GUID-keyed sidecar for much less benefit. Verified by writing every supported type, forcing the asset to disk and re-reading it from the file: all survive, including a `Sprite` reference as a real serialized reference (a JSON store in `UserSettings/` could not do that without a GUID codec, which is the main reason committed won). Every getter takes the caller's own default, so a tool with no saved state behaves exactly as before.

### Fixed
- **ZUI fill — the Size dial moved the preview and not the output (cache keyed on state the renderer no longer read).** `SplashPlayer.FillHash` decided when to re-bake the cached fill texture by hashing a hand-written list of fields — and that list read the LEGACY `zoom` scalar, while the renderer evaluates the `zoomAnim` companion which is what the Size dial actually edits. So Size never invalidated the bake, the render froze, and the editor's own swatch (uncached, calling `Evaluate` directly) kept updating: the tool showed one thing and rendered another. `gradient`/`gradientAnim` were hashed by REFERENCE too, so editing the ramp's keys did not invalidate either — the same bug, unreported. Replaced by **`ZuiFill.ContentHash(life)`**, which SAMPLES the fill instead of enumerating its fields. A field list is only ever as correct as its author's memory and fails silently when wrong; sampling cannot drift, because anything that changes the picture changes the samples and anything that does not, correctly does not re-bake.
- **TextSplash — Sprite / Noise / Grid / Dots fills rendered as a FLAT TINT.** There were two `IsSpatial` predicates: `ZuiFill.IsSpatial()` counted a texture as spatial, `SplashPlayer.IsSpatial()` excluded it (`texture == None &&`), and `ApplyMesh` gated on the player's — so a texture fill never reached the per-fragment bake and fell through to `tmp.color = fill.color`. The control built a full editing face and the swatch drew the pattern perfectly; the splash drew one colour. Grid ink, dot size/spacing, noise kind and the sprite all did nothing. The duplicate predicate is deleted rather than synced — the player now delegates to `ZuiFill.IsSpatial()`, because two predicates answering one question is the same defect as a cache keyed on the wrong field: unreviewable, only discoverable.
- **TextSplash — the BORDER fill's swatch under-promised.** It still declared a 2×2 vertex sample grid, from before spatial fills were baked; the border pass takes the same per-fragment path the face does, so a radial border washed flat in the swatch while the render drew a proper disc. (The **Sides** fill keeps its declaration — that one really is sampled per vertex.)
- **ZUI fill — the Centre pad's inert axis is now stated.** A Linear fill only moves ALONG its axis, so at 0° the Y component does nothing and at 90° the X does. Mathematically right, silently confusing; the tooltip now says which one is asleep and that rotating the angle wakes the other.
- **TextSplash — the palette lock no longer ERASES a bevel (or depth's grade).** A bevel is per-pixel lighting and depth's falloff is a grade, so both produce colours that are in no fill — and `BuildPalette` listed only the face, border and side fills, leaving the lock nothing to snap them to. Measured on a bevelled white splash: a luminance spread of 0.549 unrendered, 0.478 through the pixel buffer, and exactly **0.000** the moment palette lock came on. Pixelation was never the problem; the lock was. The palette now also carries lit and darkened variants of the author's own colours (`shadeSteps`, default 5, seeded into existing assets by a v4 migration) **only when something actually shades** — a splash with no bevel and no depth gains nothing to snap to, the same reasoning that already gates the border colour on a border being drawn. The ladder is weighted per colour by how much headroom that colour has before it clips, because a white face cannot brighten and a fixed ladder collapsed half its rungs into duplicates. Result on the same splash: 3 flat shade bands, spread 0.349, instead of one flat colour. Fewer levels than the smooth original is the *point* — that is what limited-palette shading is.
- **TextSplash — SPATIAL FILLS ARE NOW PER-FRAGMENT, so Radial actually works.** Text colour came from four vertex colours per glyph, interpolated by the GPU — which cannot express a radius. Measured: with a Stamped radial fill all four corners of a glyph land at the same distance from its centre (r = 1.308), past the end of the ramp, so all four clamped to its last colour and **the letter rendered flat**. The entire disc lived inside the quad, between the samples, where the mesh has no vertices to carry it. No centre or size value could reach it. Of the four Anchor×Fit combinations only Fixed+Uniform did anything at all, and even that only because the line is wide enough to span several glyphs' worth of samples.
  - The fill is now baked to a texture and assigned as TMP's `_FaceTex`, which `TextMeshPro/Distance Field` samples **per fragment** by uv2 — and TMP generates that uv2 itself from `horizontalMapping`/`verticalMapping`, whose `Character` and `Line` options are precisely this tool's **Stamped** and **Fixed**. So the anchor switch becomes TMP's own mapping switch and no bespoke shader is needed; the `fit` is baked into the texture. The shader must be the full Distance Field one — every Mobile variant strips `_FaceTex`, the same trap the bevel already documents. Re-baked only when an input actually moves, so a static fill costs one bake and an animated one re-bakes per frame.
  - Verified end to end by rendering through a camera: all four combinations now produce 3689–3864 distinct colours where Stamped previously produced **one**, with per-letter discs under Stamped and one gradient spanning the line under Fixed.
  - **uv2 is written per vertex rather than left to TMP's mapping**, and the baked texture is box-independent (the raw −1..1 domain). TMP's `Character` mapping stamps one texture across every glyph, so a narrow letter would wear a copy squeezed from a wider one; writing uv2 ourselves puts each glyph's OWN box through `Normalize`, so every letter gets the right aspect from the same texture. Measured per glyph under Uniform: 'A' (3.32×3.43) samples u 0.016–0.984, 'L' (2.32×3.43) samples 0.163–0.837 — each inset in proportion to its own width, which is a true circle in each. The alternative, a texture per glyph, would have cost one material and one draw call PER LETTER, since `_FaceTex` is a material property; this keeps the whole line on one of each.
- **BackSplash — TextSplash's backdrop Zoom slider and Position pad did nothing.** The backdrop blit was written three times; Pyre's and PyrePlus's copies differed only in a fallback colour, but TextSplash's had drifted invisibly — it painted the fill, the sprite and the tint and never read `imageZoom` or `imagePos`, under a comment claiming it worked "exactly like Pyre's viewport". So those two controls moved, serialized, and had no effect. All three now go through one `BackSplashPainter.Draw`; `BackSplashZui` had already made the *authoring* side single-source for the same reason. (Surveyed for a larger "preview viewport + backdrop" subsystem and deliberately did NOT build one — the painter is the only genuinely shared substance. The viewports share nothing else, Mirage has no viewport at all and draws its backdrop with a real Camera + SpriteRenderer, and Chunks/Larder/Zoetrope have no BackSplash.)
- **TextSplash — picking a backdrop image never revealed its controls.** The panel passed `EditorWindow.Repaint` as `onStructureChanged`, which does not rebuild a UITK tree, so the pad/zoom/tint row an image ADDS stayed hidden until some unrelated edit rebuilt the column. It now rebuilds just that panel. The panel also moved out of the bottom of the 400px left dial column into the right pane under the transport — beside the pixels it changes, and where Pyre and PyrePlus already put it.
- **ZUI fill — the swatch PREVIEWS THE SUBJECT, so it predicts the output.** Placement was four settings (`space`, `fit`, `zoom`, `centre`) all expressed in a normalised −1..1 box the author never saw, each redefining the others' units. The swatch painted that raw domain — a true picture of the gradient and a useless picture of the RESULT: under Uniform fit a wide text line samples a band about a tenth of the domain tall, so the only part of the swatch that predicted anything was an eight-pixel strip, and it visibly disagreed with the big preview. Each texel is now a point ON THE SUBJECT pushed through the SAME `ZuiFill.Normalize` + `Evaluate` pair the renderer runs per vertex, so the swatch IS a picture of the fill as applied, in every space/fit combination — and the combinations now differ as differences in the picture rather than as geometry the user had to infer (Fixed+Uniform on a wide line reads as vertical bands, Fixed+Stretch as one ellipse across the line; previously both drew the same circle). The consumer declares its subject with `subjectHalf`/`subjectShape`, plus `stampedHalf`/`stampedShape` for the per-repetition unit a **Stamped** fill is fitted onto (one glyph, one swarm particle), read at draw time so toggling the Anchor updates immediately. A crosshair marks where the gradient's CENTRE landed relative to the subject, degrading to an edge tick when the centre is off the subject entirely — which under Uniform fit happens long before the dial reaches 1, because the centre is measured in units of the box's LONGER side. That marker is what resolves the centre reading as reversed: moving it up moves the gradient up, so a subject that stays put shows the colours below it and appears to shift the other way. Verified by rendering the swatch beside the real laid-out "SPLASH!" glyphs coloured through the renderer's own calls, in all four combinations.
- **ZUI fill — `zoom` is now a SIZE, not a frequency (breaking semantics, migrated automatically).** The maths multiplied by `zoom`, so raising it made the gradient *smaller*, while the tooltip claimed "higher zooms the pattern in". The stored value is now the size the pattern spreads to, `Evaluate` takes its reciprocal, and a `fillVersion 0 → 1` migration inverts every existing value once on load (Static, Min-Max bounds swap, and Curve keys individually). Verified render-neutral: **zero** colour delta across every non-default zoom present in the project (24 values, all PyrePlus) in both Linear and Radial, plus idempotence under repeated loads. The dial is relabelled **Size**. The field keeps the name `zoom` on purpose — Unity matches serialized data by name, and renaming would drop every authored value in every consumer project.
